<a id='question-124'></a>

### Question 124: Renal Pathology: Focus 03-24 (Microbiology)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis and characteristic histopathological findings of acute post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis (APSGN), specifically the immune complex deposition and resulting glomerular injury. (理解化脓性链球菌感染后急性肾小球肾炎的病理生理机制和特征性组织病理学发现，特别是免疫复合物沉积和由此导致的肾小球损伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 7-year-old boy presents to the emergency department 2 weeks after recovering from a sore throat. His mother reports that he now has dark, tea-colored urine, facial puffiness, and decreased urine output. On examination, his blood pressure is 120/180 mmHg, and he has 2+ pitting edema in his lower extremities. Urinalysis reveals hematuria, proteinuria (3+), and red blood cell casts. Serum creatinine is elevated at 2.5 mg/dL (reference range 0.5-1.2 mg/dL), and C3 complement levels are markedly decreased, while C4 levels are normal. A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy shows diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis with hypercellular glomeruli due to endocapillary proliferation and infiltration by neutrophils and monocytes. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals granular deposits of IgG and C3 along the glomerular capillary walls and in the mesangium, forming a characteristic "starry sky" pattern. Electron microscopy confirms subepithelial "humps." Which of the following findings is most characteristic of the underlying pathogenetic mechanism in this patient?

(A) Deposition of linear IgG along the glomerular basement membrane.
(B) Formation of immune complexes in situ within the glomerular mesangium.
(C) Granular deposition of immune complexes in the subendothelial space.
(D) Formation of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies.
(E) Deposition of granular immune complexes predominantly in the mesangium and subepithelial space ("humps").

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 7-year-old boy, suggesting pediatric conditions.
- **Timing:** 2 weeks after a sore throat (likely streptococcal pharyngitis), a classic latency period for APSGN.
- **Symptoms:** Dark urine (hematuria), facial puffiness, decreased urine output (oliguria), edema – classic signs of nephritic syndrome.
- **Vital Signs:** Hypertension (120/180 mmHg) – common in nephritic syndrome due to fluid retention and renin-angiotensin system activation.
- **Urinalysis:** Hematuria, proteinuria (3+), red blood cell casts – hallmark of glomerular inflammation/damage. RBC casts specifically point to glomerular origin of hematuria.
- **Labs:** Elevated creatinine (2.5 mg/dL) indicates acute kidney injury (AKI). Decreased C3, normal C4 – characteristic of complement consumption via the alternative pathway, typical for immune complex-mediated diseases like APSGN.
- **Renal Biopsy (Light Microscopy):** Diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis, hypercellular glomeruli, endocapillary proliferation, neutrophil/monocyte infiltration – features of acute glomerular inflammation.
- **Renal Biopsy (Immunofluorescence):** Granular deposits of IgG and C3 along capillary walls and mesangium ("starry sky") – indicates immune complex deposition.
- **Renal Biopsy (Electron Microscopy):** Subepithelial "humps" – pathognomonic for APSGN, representing electron-dense deposits.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the characteristic finding of the *underlying pathogenetic mechanism*. The mechanism is immune complex deposition following streptococcal infection. The biopsy findings (granular IF, subepithelial humps) directly reflect this mechanism.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Pathogenesis of APSGN:** Occurs after infection with certain nephritogenic strains of Group A Streptococcus (GAS). Streptococcal antigens (e.g., streptococcal pyrogenic exotoxins, enolase, plasminogen activator) circulate in the bloodstream and form immune complexes with host antibodies (primarily IgG).
- **Immune Complex Deposition:** These circulating immune complexes deposit in the glomeruli, particularly in the subepithelial space, mesangium, and sometimes subendothelial space.
- **Complement Activation:** The deposited immune complexes activate the complement system, primarily via the alternative pathway (hence low C3, normal C4).
- **Inflammation:** Complement activation leads to the recruitment of inflammatory cells (neutrophils, monocytes) into the glomeruli, releasing proteases and reactive oxygen species.
- **Glomerular Injury:** This inflammatory cascade causes damage to the glomerular capillaries, basement membrane, podocytes, and mesangial cells, leading to the clinical manifestations of nephritic syndrome (hematuria, proteinuria, edema, hypertension, AKI).
- **Biopsy Findings:** The characteristic biopsy findings (diffuse proliferation, hypercellularity, neutrophils, granular IF deposits, subepithelial humps on EM) are direct consequences of this immune complex-mediated inflammatory process. The question asks for the characteristic finding of this mechanism, which is the *pattern* of immune complex deposition seen on biopsy. Option E accurately describes this pattern: granular deposition in the mesangium and subepithelial space ("humps").

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Deposition of linear IgG along the glomerular basement membrane. This pattern is characteristic of Goodpasture syndrome (anti-GBM disease), where antibodies directly target the GBM. This patient has granular deposits, not linear, and the clinical presentation (post-streptococcal infection, low C3) is inconsistent with Goodpasture syndrome.
- (B): Formation of immune complexes in situ within the glomerular mesangium. This mechanism is characteristic of IgA nephropathy (Berger's disease), where IgA-containing immune complexes form locally in the mesangium. While mesangial deposition occurs in APSGN, the primary deposition site and the presence of subepithelial humps are more characteristic, and IgA is not the predominant immunoglobulin.
- (C): Granular deposition of immune complexes in the subendothelial space. While some subendothelial deposition can occur in APSGN, the *most characteristic* pattern involves the mesangium and subepithelial space (humps). Predominant subendothelial deposition is more typical of membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN) type I or lupus nephritis class III/IV.
- (D): Formation of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies. This is the mechanism underlying Goodpasture syndrome and rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis (RPGN) associated with anti-GBM disease. As discussed in (A), the biopsy shows granular, not linear, deposits, ruling this out.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | APSGN (Post-Streptococcal GN) | Goodpasture Syndrome (Anti-GBM) | IgA Nephropathy | Membranoproliferative GN (MPGN Type I) | Lupus Nephritis (Class IV) |
|-------------------------|---------------------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------|---------------------------------------|-----------------------------|
| **Trigger**             | Strep infection                 | Autoimmune (Anti-GBM Ab)       | Unknown        | Immune complex (often Hepatitis C)    | Systemic Lupus Erythematosus |
| **Latency**             | 1-3 weeks                       | Variable                       | Variable       | Variable                             | Variable                   |
| **Clinical**            | Nephritic syndrome              | Nephritic/RPGN                  | Nephritic/Hematuria | Nephritic/Nephrotic                   | Variable                   |
| **Complement (C3/C4)** | Low C3, Normal C4              | Normal                         | Normal         | Low C3, Normal C4 (often)            | Variable                   |
| **IF Microscopy**       | Granular IgG, C3 ("starry sky") | Linear IgG along GBM           | Mesangial IgA  | Granular IgG, C3 (subendothelial)    | Variable (granular)         |
| **EM**                  | Subepithelial "humps"           | None                           | Mesangial deposits | Subendothelial deposits, GBM spikes | Variable (subendothelial/mesangial) |
| **Pathogenesis**        | Circulating immune complexes    | Anti-GBM antibodies             | In situ IgA complexes | Circulating immune complexes          | Circulating immune complexes |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
APSGN is a classic example of immune complex-mediated glomerulonephritis following a streptococcal infection. The hallmark biopsy findings are diffuse proliferative changes, granular IgG and C3 deposition ("starry sky" pattern on IF), and subepithelial electron-dense deposits ("humps" on EM). Remember to differentiate it from other causes of nephritic syndrome based on clinical context (post-infection), complement levels (low C3), and specific biopsy findings (linear IgG in anti-GBM disease, mesangial IgA in IgA nephropathy).

<a id='question-125'></a>

### Question 125: Renal Pathology: Henoch-Schönlein Purpura (HSP)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: IgA deposition in renal mesangium leading to glomerulonephritis in the context of systemic vasculitis (IgA 血管炎性肾小球肾炎).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 7-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of palpable purpura on his lower extremities and buttocks, joint pain (arthralgia) in his knees and ankles, and abdominal pain. His mother reports he had a recent upper respiratory infection. Physical examination reveals a febrile child (38.5°C) with scattered purpuric lesions, mild edema, and tenderness in the lower abdomen. Urinalysis shows hematuria (microscopic) and proteinuria (1+). Serum creatinine is normal (0.5 mg/dL). A skin biopsy from a purpuric lesion shows leukocytoclastic vasculitis with fibrinoid necrosis and neutrophilic infiltrate. Immunofluorescence microscopy of the skin biopsy reveals granular deposition of IgA in the dermal blood vessels. Given the constellation of symptoms and findings, what is the most likely underlying pathological process affecting the kidneys in this patient?

(A) Deposition of immune complexes containing IgG and C3 in the subepithelial space, leading to a "spike and dome" appearance on electron microscopy.
(B) Formation of subendothelial immune deposits containing IgG and C1q, often associated with cryoglobulinemia and membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN).
(C) Mesangial proliferation and deposition of IgA-containing immune complexes, leading to crescent formation in severe cases.
(D) Linear deposition of IgG along the glomerular basement membrane (GBM), characteristic of anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture syndrome).
(E) Deposition of C3 fragments in the mesangium and along the GBM, often associated with alternative pathway complement activation and nephrotic syndrome.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 7-year-old boy - HSP is most common in children.
- **Symptoms:** Palpable purpura (lower extremities/buttocks), arthralgia (knees/ankles), abdominal pain, recent URI - Classic tetrad of HSP.
- **Physical Exam:** Febrile, purpura, edema, abdominal tenderness - Consistent with systemic inflammation and potential renal involvement.
- **Urinalysis:** Hematuria, proteinuria - Suggests glomerular injury. Normal creatinine indicates preserved renal function currently.
- **Skin Biopsy:** Leukocytoclastic vasculitis with IgA deposition - Confirms HSP diagnosis and the characteristic IgA involvement.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most likely underlying pathological process affecting the kidneys*. This requires linking the systemic diagnosis (HSP) to its specific renal manifestation.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- HSP is a systemic vasculitis characterized by IgA-dominant immune complex deposition.
- The classic presentation involves skin (palpable purpura), joints (arthralgia/arthritis), GI tract (abdominal pain), and kidneys (glomerulonephritis).
- The renal involvement in HSP is IgA nephropathy (Berger's disease).
- Pathologically, this involves mesangial proliferation (increase in mesangial cells and matrix) and deposition of IgA-containing immune complexes within the mesangium.
- Electron microscopy shows electron-dense deposits in the mesangium.
- Immunofluorescence microscopy shows granular deposition of IgA in the mesangium, often accompanied by C3 and sometimes IgG or IgM.
- Severe cases can lead to crescent formation (proliferation of parietal epithelial cells and infiltration of inflammatory cells into Bowman's space), indicating rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis.
- The mechanism involves abnormal IgA1 glycosylation, leading to the formation of immune complexes that deposit in small vessels (skin, gut) and the glomeruli.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Describes membranous nephropathy. Characterized by IgG and C3 deposition in the subepithelial space, leading to GBM thickening and "spike and dome" appearance on EM. Typically presents with nephrotic syndrome, not the systemic vasculitis picture of HSP.
- (B): Describes MPGN type I (immune complex-mediated) or cryoglobulinemic glomerulonephritis. Characterized by subendothelial deposits containing IgG and C1q. Often associated with systemic diseases like infections (HCV) or autoimmune disorders. While it involves immune complexes, the specific location (subendothelial) and immune components (IgG, C1q) are incorrect for HSP nephritis.
- (D): Describes anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture syndrome). Characterized by linear deposition of IgG along the GBM. Presents with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and often pulmonary hemorrhage. The deposition pattern (linear vs. granular) and target antigen (GBM vs. mesangium) are incorrect for HSP nephritis.
- (E): Describes C3 glomerulopathy (C3G) or dense deposit disease (DDD). Characterized by dominant C3 deposition in the mesangium and along the GBM, often with electron-dense deposits on EM. Associated with alternative complement pathway dysregulation. While C3 is often co-deposited in HSP nephritis, the primary and defining deposit is IgA, not C3 alone.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | HSP Nephritis (IgA Nephropathy) | Membranous Nephropathy | MPGN Type I | Anti-GBM Disease | C3 Glomerulopathy |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :--------------------- | :---------- | :--------------- | :---------------- |
| **Primary Deposit**     | IgA (mesangium)                | IgG, C3 (subepithelial) | IgG, C1q (subendothelial) | IgG (linear GBM) | C3 (mesangium/GBM) |
| **Deposition Pattern**  | Granular (mesangial)            | Granular (subepithelial) | Granular (subendothelial) | Linear (GBM)     | Granular (mesangial/GBM) |
| **EM Appearance**       | Mesangial deposits             | Spikes & Domes         | Subendothelial deposits | Linear GBM thickening | Dense deposits (DDD) |
| **Clinical Context**    | Systemic vasculitis (purpura, arthritis, abd pain) | Nephrotic syndrome | Variable (nephrotic/nephritic) | RPGN, pulm hemorrhage | Variable (nephrotic/nephritic) |
| **Associated Diseases** | URI, IgA deficiency            | None specific          | HCV, cryoglobulinemia | None specific    | Alternative complement pathway defects |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
HSP nephritis is essentially IgA nephropathy occurring in the context of systemic HSP. The key pathological finding is mesangial IgA deposition. Differentiating it from other glomerulonephritides relies on understanding the specific immune deposits, their location, and the associated clinical syndrome. Remember the classic HSP tetrad and the characteristic IgA deposition pattern.

<a id='question-126'></a>

### Question 126: Renal Pathology: Focus 03-26 (• Systemic childhood disorder)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology, clinical presentation, and diagnostic findings of Henoch-Schönlein Purpura (HSP), a systemic vasculitis common in children, specifically focusing on its renal manifestations (HSP nephritis).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 7-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of palpable purpura on his lower extremities and buttocks, joint pain (arthralgia) in his knees and ankles, and abdominal pain. His mother reports he had a recent upper respiratory infection. On examination, he has a low-grade fever (37.8°C), blood pressure of 100/60 mmHg, and the characteristic purpuric rash. Laboratory tests show normal complete blood count, normal coagulation studies, and elevated serum creatinine (1.8 mg/dL, reference range 0.5-1.2 mg/dL). Urinalysis reveals hematuria (microscopic) and proteinuria (2+). A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy shows diffuse mesangial proliferation with neutrophils and red blood cells infiltrating the glomeruli. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals granular deposits of IgA in the mesangium. Electron microscopy shows electron-dense deposits in the mesangium. Which of the following is the most likely underlying pathological mechanism responsible for the patient's renal findings?

(A) Deposition of subepithelial immune complexes leading to glomerular basement membrane thickening and "spike and dome" appearance on silver stain.
(B) Formation of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies causing linear IgG deposition along the GBM.
(C) Immune complex deposition in the mesangium, triggering complement activation and inflammatory cell infiltration.
(D) Formation of cryoglobulins leading to membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN) with subendothelial and mesangial deposits.
(E) Deposition of amyloid fibrils in the glomeruli, causing Congo red staining and apple-green birefringence under polarized light.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 7-year-old boy - HSP is most common in children.
*   **Symptoms:** Palpable purpura (lower extremities/buttocks), arthralgia (knees/ankles), abdominal pain, recent URI - Classic tetrad of HSP.
*   **Vital Signs/Exam:** Low-grade fever, normal BP (initially), purpuric rash - Consistent with HSP.
*   **Labs:** Elevated creatinine (1.8 mg/dL) - Indicates acute kidney injury (AKI).
*   **Urinalysis:** Hematuria, proteinuria - Suggests glomerular involvement (nephritis).
*   **Renal Biopsy:**
    *   Light Microscopy: Diffuse mesangial proliferation with neutrophils/RBCs - Hallmark of HSP nephritis.
    *   Immunofluorescence: Granular IgA deposits in the mesangium - Diagnostic feature of IgA nephropathy, which is the renal manifestation of HSP.
    *   Electron Microscopy: Electron-dense deposits in the mesangium - Confirms immune complex deposition.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying pathological mechanism.

The clinical presentation (purpura, arthritis, abdominal pain, recent URI in a child) strongly suggests Henoch-Schönlein Purpura (HSP). The renal biopsy findings (mesangial proliferation, IgA deposition) confirm HSP nephritis, which is essentially IgA nephropathy occurring in the context of systemic HSP. The core mechanism involves IgA immune complex deposition in the mesangium, leading to inflammation and glomerular damage.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **HSP Pathogenesis:** HSP is an IgA-mediated systemic vasculitis. The exact trigger is unknown, but it often follows an infection (like URI). Aberrant IgA1 glycosylation leads to the formation of circulating IgA immune complexes.
*   **Renal Involvement (HSP Nephritis):** These IgA complexes deposit predominantly in the glomerular mesangium.
*   **Mechanism:** The deposited IgA activates the alternative complement pathway (though classical pathway activation can also occur), leading to the release of inflammatory mediators (C3a, C5a). This recruits neutrophils and monocytes into the glomeruli, causing mesangial cell proliferation, endothelial cell injury, and ultimately glomerular damage, resulting in hematuria, proteinuria, and potentially renal failure.
*   **Biopsy Findings Correlation:** The biopsy findings directly reflect this mechanism:
    *   Mesangial proliferation: Response to inflammation and immune complex deposition.
    *   Neutrophils/RBCs: Inflammatory infiltrate.
    *   IgA deposition (IF): The primary immune complex involved.
    *   Electron-dense deposits (EM): Visual confirmation of immune complexes.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Deposition of subepithelial immune complexes leading to glomerular basement membrane thickening and "spike and dome" appearance on silver stain.** This describes membranous nephropathy, typically caused by antibodies against the phospholipase A2 receptor (PLA2R) or THSD7A, leading to subepithelial deposits. It does not fit the clinical picture of HSP or the biopsy findings (mesangial, not subepithelial deposits; IgA, not anti-PLA2R).
*   **(B) Formation of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies causing linear IgG deposition along the GBM.** This describes anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture syndrome). It presents with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and often pulmonary hemorrhage. The immunofluorescence pattern is linear IgG along the GBM, not granular mesangial IgA.
*   **(D) Formation of cryoglobulins leading to membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN) with subendothelial and mesangial deposits.** Cryoglobulinemia (often associated with Hepatitis C) can cause MPGN. While it involves immune complex deposition, the pattern is typically subendothelial and mesangial, and the clinical presentation (HSP tetrad) is different. The specific IgA deposition seen in HSP nephritis is not characteristic of cryoglobulinemic MPGN.
*   **(E) Deposition of amyloid fibrils in the glomeruli, causing Congo red staining and apple-green birefringence under polarized light.** This describes amyloidosis affecting the kidney. Amyloid deposits are extracellular, stain with Congo red, and show apple-green birefringence. This is unrelated to the IgA-mediated vasculitis seen in HSP.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | HSP Nephritis (IgA Nephropathy) | Membranous Nephropathy | Anti-GBM Disease | Cryoglobulinemic MPGN | Amyloidosis |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------ | :--------------------- | :--------------- | :-------------------- | :---------- |
| **Clinical Context**    | Child, Purpura, Arthritis, Abdominal Pain | Often adult, Nephrotic Syndrome | RPGN, +/- Pulmonary Hemorrhage | Hepatitis C, Vasculitis | Systemic disease |
| **Immune Deposit**      | IgA (mesangial)                 | IgG (subepithelial)    | IgG (linear GBM) | IgM/IgG (subendothelial/mesangial) | Amyloid fibrils |
| **IF Pattern**          | Granular mesangial IgA           | Granular subepithelial IgG | Linear IgG GBM  | Granular IgM/IgG     | Congo Red (+) |
| **EM**                  | Mesangial electron-dense deposits | Subepithelial electron-dense deposits, "spikes" | None (GBM damage)    | Subendothelial/mesangial deposits | Fibrils |
| **Light Microscopy**    | Mesangial proliferation         | GBM thickening        | Crescent formation | MPGN pattern         | Amyloid deposits |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Henoch-Schönlein Purpura (HSP) is an IgA-mediated systemic vasculitis common in children, characterized by purpura, arthritis, abdominal pain, and often renal involvement (HSP nephritis). The renal biopsy shows mesangial IgA deposition, reflecting the underlying immune complex-mediated mechanism. Differentiating HSP nephritis from other glomerulonephritides relies on recognizing the characteristic clinical presentation and the specific immunofluorescence pattern (granular mesangial IgA). Key differentials include IgA nephropathy without systemic symptoms, post-infectious glomerulonephritis (often subepithelial humps), membranous nephropathy, anti-GBM disease, and MPGN.

<a id='question-127'></a>

### Question 127: Renal Pathology: Acute Post-Streptococcal Glomerulonephritis (APSGN)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the clinical presentation, pathogenesis, and characteristic histological findings of Acute Post-Streptococcal Glomerulonephritis (APSGN), specifically the immune complex deposition and glomerular injury following a streptococcal infection. (本题考察了链球菌感染后急性肾小球肾炎 (APSGN) 的临床表现、发病机制和特征性组织学发现，特别是免疫复合物沉积和肾小球损伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 7-year-old boy presents to the emergency department 2 weeks after recovering from a sore throat. His mother reports that he now has dark, tea-colored urine, facial puffiness, and decreased urine output. On examination, his blood pressure is 110/70 mmHg, heart rate is 90 bpm, and respiratory rate is 20 bpm. He has periorbital edema and 2+ pitting edema in his lower extremities. Urinalysis reveals hematuria (RBCs >50/HPF), proteinuria (3+), and hyaline casts. Serum creatinine is elevated at 1.8 mg/dL (reference range 0.5-1.0 mg/dL), and a throat culture performed 2 weeks prior grew Group A Streptococcus. A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy shows diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis with enlarged, hypercellular glomeruli. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals granular deposits of IgG and C3 along the glomerular basement membrane (GBM) and in the mesangium in a "starry sky" pattern. Electron microscopy shows subepithelial electron-dense deposits, often described as "humps".

What is the most likely underlying pathological mechanism responsible for the glomerular injury in this patient?

(A) Deposition of circulating immune complexes containing streptococcal antigens, leading to complement activation and inflammatory cell infiltration.
(B) Formation of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies directly targeting the GBM, causing linear IgG deposition.
(C) In situ immune complex formation within the glomerulus due to planted antigens, leading to mesangial proliferation and IgA deposition.
(D) Deposition of pre-formed antibodies against podocyte antigens, leading to podocyte injury and nephrotic syndrome.
(E) Complement-mediated injury due to alternative pathway activation, resulting in C3 deposition without significant immune complex deposition.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 7-year-old boy. APSGN commonly affects children.
- **Timing:** Presentation 2 weeks after a sore throat (Group A Streptococcus infection). This latency period is characteristic of APSGN.
- **Symptoms:** Dark urine (hematuria), facial puffiness (edema), decreased urine output (oliguria). These are classic signs of acute nephritic syndrome.
- **Physical Exam:** Periorbital edema, lower extremity edema, normal blood pressure (though hypertension is common, it can be normal initially).
- **Urinalysis:** Hematuria (>50 RBCs/HPF), proteinuria (3+), hyaline casts. Consistent with nephritic syndrome.
- **Labs:** Elevated serum creatinine (1.8 mg/dL), indicating acute kidney injury. Positive throat culture for Group A Streptococcus confirms the preceding infection.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:**
    - **Light Microscopy:** Diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis (hypercellular glomeruli).
    - **Immunofluorescence:** Granular IgG and C3 deposits along GBM and mesangium ("starry sky"). This pattern indicates immune complex deposition.
    - **Electron Microscopy:** Subepithelial "humps" (electron-dense deposits). These are characteristic of APSGN.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying pathological mechanism.

The combination of recent streptococcal infection, nephritic syndrome presentation, and the specific biopsy findings (granular immune complex deposition, subepithelial humps) strongly points to APSGN. The mechanism involves circulating immune complexes formed between streptococcal antigens and host antibodies. These complexes deposit in the glomeruli, activate complement, attract inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages), and cause glomerular damage.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **APSGN Pathogenesis:** Following a Group A Streptococcus infection (pharyngitis or impetigo), the body produces antibodies against streptococcal antigens. These antibodies bind to circulating streptococcal antigens, forming immune complexes.
- **Immune Complex Deposition:** These circulating immune complexes deposit in the glomerular capillaries, particularly in the subepithelial space (leading to the "humps" seen on EM) and the mesangium.
- **Complement Activation:** The deposited immune complexes activate the classical complement pathway, leading to the deposition of C3 and C5b-9 (membrane attack complex).
- **Inflammation:** Complement activation products (e.g., C5a) are chemotactic for neutrophils and macrophages. These inflammatory cells infiltrate the glomeruli, releasing proteases and reactive oxygen species, causing further damage to the glomerular basement membrane, podocytes, and mesangial cells.
- **Glomerular Injury:** The inflammation and cellular proliferation lead to glomerular hypercellularity (proliferation of endothelial, mesangial, and infiltrating inflammatory cells), damage to the GBM, and reduced glomerular filtration rate (GFR), resulting in hematuria, proteinuria, edema, hypertension, and oliguria (nephritic syndrome).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** This option accurately describes the pathogenesis of APSGN: circulating immune complexes containing streptococcal antigens deposit in the glomeruli, activate complement, and trigger inflammation. This aligns perfectly with the clinical presentation and biopsy findings (latency period, nephritic syndrome, granular IF, subepithelial humps).
- (B): **Incorrect.** This describes the mechanism of Goodpasture syndrome (anti-GBM disease). It involves autoantibodies directly targeting the alpha-3 chain of type IV collagen in the GBM, leading to *linear* IgG deposition on IF, not granular. Goodpasture syndrome typically presents with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and pulmonary hemorrhage.
- (C): **Incorrect.** This describes the mechanism of IgA nephropathy (Berger's disease). It involves the deposition of IgA-containing immune complexes primarily in the mesangium, often triggered by mucosal infections (like URI), but the dominant immunoglobulin is IgA, not IgG, and the deposits are typically mesangial, not subepithelial humps. The IF pattern is granular, but the dominant Ig is IgA.
- (D): **Incorrect.** This describes the mechanism of certain forms of membranous nephropathy or focal segmental glomerulosclerosis (FSGS), particularly those associated with anti-podocyte antibodies (e.g., collapsing glomerulopathy). These conditions typically present with nephrotic syndrome (heavy proteinuria, hypoalbuminemia, edema, hyperlipidemia) rather than nephritic syndrome, and the primary injury targets podocytes, leading to foot process effacement. While immune complexes might be involved, the primary target and clinical presentation differ significantly.
- (E): **Incorrect.** This describes a mechanism sometimes seen in C3 glomerulopathy (C3G) or dense deposit disease (DDD). These conditions involve dysregulation of the alternative complement pathway, leading to excessive C3 deposition (often dense deposits on EM) without significant immune complex deposition (IF may show C3 but minimal or no IgG/IgA). While complement activation is central to APSGN, the primary trigger is immune complex deposition, not primary alternative pathway dysregulation.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | APSGN (Option A)                                  | Anti-GBM (Option B)                               | IgA Nephropathy (Option C)                           | Anti-Podocyte Ab (Option D)                       | C3G/DDD (Option E)                               |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------ |
| **Trigger**             | Group A Strep infection                          | Autoimmune (anti-GBM Ab)                          | URI, mucosal infection                              | Autoimmune (anti-podocyte Ab)                     | Complement dysregulation                         |
| **Immune Complex Type** | Circulating (Streptococcal Ag + Host Ab)         | None (Direct Ab attack)                            | Circulating (IgA + Ag)                               | Variable (may involve immune complexes)           | Minimal/None (primarily complement)              |
| **IF Pattern**          | Granular IgG, C3 (GBM, Mesangium)                 | Linear IgG (along GBM)                             | Granular IgA (Mesangium)                             | Variable (may show IgG, C3)                       | Granular C3 (often dense deposits on EM)          |
| **EM Findings**         | Subepithelial "humps"                             | GBM thickening, no specific deposits               | Mesangial deposits                                   | Podocyte effacement, GBM changes                 | Dense deposits (DDD), C3 deposits (C3G)          |
| **Clinical Syndrome**   | Nephritic (hematuria, proteinuria, edema, HTN)     | Rapidly Progressive Glomerulonephritis, Lung Hemorrhage | Nephritic (often recurrent hematuria after URI)      | Nephrotic (heavy proteinuria)                     | Nephritic or Nephrotic                             |
| **Latency Period**      | 1-3 weeks after infection                         | None (acute)                                       | Variable                                             | Variable                                          | Variable                                          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute Post-Streptococcal Glomerulonephritis (APSGN) is a classic example of immune complex-mediated glomerulonephritis following a streptococcal infection. The hallmark is the deposition of granular IgG and C3 immune complexes in the glomeruli, often visualized as subepithelial "humps" on electron microscopy. Key differentials include other causes of nephritic syndrome (IgA nephropathy, anti-GBM disease) and nephrotic syndrome (membranous nephropathy, FSGS). Remember the characteristic latency period after infection and the specific biopsy findings for APSGN.

<a id='question-128'></a>

### Question 128: Renal Pathology: IgA Nephropathy
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: IgA nephropathy (Berger's disease) is characterized by the deposition of IgA immune complexes in the glomerular mesangium, leading to glomerular inflammation and damage. The definitive diagnosis relies on demonstrating mesangial IgA deposition via immunofluorescence microscopy on a renal biopsy. (IgA肾病（伯杰病）的特点是IgA免疫复合物沉积在肾小球系膜中，导致肾小球炎症和损伤。确诊依赖于通过肾活检的免疫荧光显微镜检查证实系膜IgA沉积。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department 2 days after experiencing macroscopic hematuria following an upper respiratory tract infection. He reports no flank pain, fever, or dysuria. His past medical history is unremarkable, and he denies any history of hypertension or diabetes. Physical examination reveals mild edema in the lower extremities. Urinalysis shows significant hematuria (RBC > 50/HPF) and mild proteinuria (1+). Serum creatinine is 1.4 mg/dL (reference range: 0.6-1.2 mg/dL), and serum albumin is 3.8 g/dL (reference range: 3.5-5.0 g/dL). A renal biopsy is performed. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals granular deposits of IgA in the mesangium. Light microscopy shows mesangial cell proliferation and endocapillary hypercellularity. Electron microscopy shows electron-dense deposits within the mesangium.

Which of the following findings is most characteristic of this patient's condition?

(A) Subepithelial immune complex deposits ("humps") seen on electron microscopy.
(B) Linear deposition of IgG along the glomerular basement membrane seen on immunofluorescence microscopy.
(C) Podocyte effacement seen on electron microscopy.
(D) Mesangial IgA deposition seen on immunofluorescence microscopy.
(E) Granular deposits of C3 along the glomerular basement membrane seen on immunofluorescence microscopy.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male, relatively young adult.
- **Presenting Complaint:** Macroscopic hematuria (visible blood in urine) 2 days after an upper respiratory tract infection (URI). This temporal association (synpharyngitic hematuria) is a classic feature.
- **Symptoms:** No flank pain, fever, or dysuria, making acute pyelonephritis or nephrolithiasis less likely.
- **Physical Exam:** Mild lower extremity edema, suggesting some degree of renal dysfunction or fluid retention.
- **Urinalysis:** Significant hematuria (>50 RBC/HPF) and mild proteinuria (1+). Consistent with glomerular injury.
- **Labs:** Elevated serum creatinine (1.4 mg/dL) indicates acute kidney injury (AKI). Normal serum albumin (3.8 g/dL) suggests proteinuria is not severe enough to cause significant hypoalbuminemia yet.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:**
    - Immunofluorescence: Granular deposits of IgA in the mesangium. This is the hallmark finding.
    - Light Microscopy: Mesangial cell proliferation and endocapillary hypercellularity. Indicative of glomerular inflammation.
    - Electron Microscopy: Electron-dense deposits within the mesangium. Correlates with the IgA deposits seen on immunofluorescence.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most characteristic* finding of this patient's condition, given the clinical presentation and biopsy results.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **IgA Nephropathy Pathogenesis:** The exact pathogenesis is not fully understood, but it involves the abnormal glycosylation of IgA1, leading to the formation of polymeric IgA1 complexes that deposit in the glomerular mesangium. These deposits trigger complement activation (alternative pathway) and inflammation, leading to glomerular damage.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Often presents as recurrent macroscopic hematuria, typically occurring 1-3 days after a mucosal infection (e.g., URI, gastroenteritis). Microscopic hematuria and proteinuria may be present between episodes. Progression can lead to chronic kidney disease (CKD) and end-stage renal disease (ESRD).
- **Diagnosis:** Definitive diagnosis requires a renal biopsy demonstrating mesangial IgA deposition on immunofluorescence microscopy.
- **Biopsy Findings:**
    - **Light Microscopy:** Variable findings, often showing mesangial hypercellularity, endocapillary proliferation, and sometimes crescent formation in severe cases.
    - **Immunofluorescence:** The key finding is granular deposition of IgA in the mesangium. C3 may also be present, often co-localizing with IgA. IgG and IgM are typically absent or minimal.
    - **Electron Microscopy:** Electron-dense deposits within the mesangium.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Subepithelial immune complex deposits ("humps") seen on electron microscopy. This finding is characteristic of post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis (PSGN), which typically presents with hematuria, edema, and hypertension after a streptococcal infection. The deposits are located on the epithelial side of the glomerular basement membrane (GBM). This patient's deposits are mesangial.
- (B) Linear deposition of IgG along the glomerular basement membrane seen on immunofluorescence microscopy. This is the hallmark of anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture syndrome), an autoimmune disease targeting the alpha-3 chain of type IV collagen in the GBM. It presents with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis (RPGN) and often pulmonary hemorrhage. The deposition pattern is linear, not granular, and involves IgG, not IgA.
- (C) Podocyte effacement seen on electron microscopy. This finding, also known as foot process fusion, is characteristic of nephrotic syndrome, particularly minimal change disease (MCD) and focal segmental glomerulosclerosis (FSGS). While proteinuria can occur in IgA nephropathy, podocyte effacement is not the primary or most characteristic finding. MCD typically shows normal light microscopy and immunofluorescence. FSGS shows segmental sclerosis on light microscopy.
- (D) Mesangial IgA deposition seen on immunofluorescence microscopy. This is the defining diagnostic feature of IgA nephropathy (Berger's disease). The clinical presentation (synpharyngitic hematuria in a young adult) and the biopsy findings described (mesangial IgA deposits, mesangial proliferation, mesangial electron-dense deposits) are all consistent with this diagnosis.
- (E) Granular deposits of C3 along the glomerular basement membrane seen on immunofluorescence microscopy. Granular C3 deposition along the GBM can be seen in various conditions, including membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN) and lupus nephritis. While C3 can co-deposit with IgA in the mesangium in IgA nephropathy, the primary and characteristic finding is IgA deposition, and the location is typically mesangial, not along the GBM.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                     | IgA Nephropathy (Berger's) | Post-Streptococcal GN | Anti-GBM Disease | Minimal Change Disease | Membranoproliferative GN |
| :-------------------------- | :------------------------- | :-------------------- | :--------------- | :--------------------- | :----------------------- |
| **Typical Presentation**     | Synpharyngitic hematuria   | Post-strep infection  | RPGN, pulm hem.  | Nephrotic syndrome     | Nephrotic/Nephritic      |
| **Age**                     | Young adult                | Children/Young adult  | Any age          | Children               | Any age                 |
| **Immunofluorescence**      | Mesangial IgA              | Subepithelial IgG/C3  | Linear IgG       | Negative               | Granular C3 (GBM/Mes)    |
| **Electron Microscopy**     | Mesangial deposits         | Subepithelial humps   | None             | Podocyte effacement    | Subendothelial/Mesangial |
| **Light Microscopy**        | Mesangial hypercellularity | Diffuse proliferation | Crescentic GN    | Normal                 | Thickened GBM, Hypercel. |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
IgA nephropathy is the most common primary glomerulonephritis worldwide, classically presenting with macroscopic hematuria following an upper respiratory infection. The definitive diagnosis relies on demonstrating mesangial IgA deposition on renal biopsy immunofluorescence. Key differentials include PSGN (subepithelial humps), anti-GBM disease (linear IgG), and other causes of glomerulonephritis. (IgA肾病是全球最常见的原发性肾小球肾炎，经典表现为上呼吸道感染后出现的大便血。确诊依赖于肾活检免疫荧光显示系膜IgA沉积。主要鉴别包括链球菌感染后肾小球肾炎（上皮下驼峰）、抗GBM病（线性IgG）和其他肾小球肾炎。)

<a id='question-129'></a>

### Question 129: Renal Pathology: Focus 03-29 (• Abdominal pain)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology and histological features of acute tubular necrosis (ATN), specifically ischemic ATN, and differentiating it from other causes of acute kidney injury (AKI) presenting with abdominal pain and flank tenderness.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of hypertension, type 2 diabetes mellitus, and chronic kidney disease (stage 3b) presents to the emergency department complaining of acute onset severe left flank pain radiating to the groin, accompanied by nausea and vomiting. He reports that his urine output has decreased significantly over the past 24 hours. His vital signs show a blood pressure of 85/50 mmHg, heart rate of 110 bpm, respiratory rate of 22 breaths/min, and temperature of 37.2°C. Physical examination reveals tenderness to palpation over the left costovertebral angle. Laboratory results show: BUN 65 mg/dL (normal 7-20), Creatinine 4.2 mg/dL (normal 0.6-1.2), Potassium 6.8 mEq/L (normal 3.5-5.0), Hemoglobin 9.5 g/dL (normal 13.5-17.5), Platelets 150,000/µL (normal 150,000-400,000), and urinalysis shows muddy brown casts. An abdominal CT scan without contrast reveals bilateral enlarged kidneys with perinephric stranding and no evidence of obstruction. Given the patient's presentation and laboratory findings, which of the following is the most likely underlying pathological process?

(A) Acute interstitial nephritis (AIN) secondary to NSAID use
(B) Contrast-induced nephropathy (CIN) following recent CT angiography
(C) Ischemic acute tubular necrosis (ATN) secondary to hypovolemic shock
(D) Glomerulonephritis associated with systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE)
(E) Acute pyelonephritis complicated by renal abscess formation

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with comorbidities (HTN, DM2, CKD stage 3b) - increases risk for AKI and cardiovascular events.
*   **Presenting Symptoms:** Acute severe flank pain, nausea, vomiting, decreased urine output (oliguria). Flank pain can suggest renal pathology, obstruction, or ischemia.
*   **Vital Signs:** Hypotension (BP 85/50 mmHg) and tachycardia (HR 110 bpm) strongly suggest hypovolemic shock or severe dehydration.
*   **Physical Exam:** Left CVA tenderness points towards a left renal issue, but the CT shows bilateral findings.
*   **Laboratory Findings:**
    *   Elevated BUN (65 mg/dL) and Creatinine (4.2 mg/dL) indicate acute kidney injury (AKI).
    *   Hyperkalemia (6.8 mEq/L) is a common complication of AKI, especially ATN.
    *   Anemia (Hgb 9.5 g/dL) could be chronic (CKD) or acute (e.g., bleeding, hemolysis, though less likely here).
    *   Urinalysis: Muddy brown casts are pathognomonic for ATN.
*   **Imaging:** Abdominal CT without contrast shows bilateral enlarged kidneys and perinephric stranding. Bilateral enlargement suggests intrinsic renal disease or ischemia, while perinephric stranding indicates inflammation or edema. Lack of obstruction rules out post-renal AKI.
*   **Synthesis:** The patient presents with AKI (elevated BUN/Cr, oliguria), signs of hypovolemic shock (hypotension, tachycardia), flank pain, and muddy brown casts in the urine. The CT findings support bilateral intrinsic renal injury without obstruction. The combination of shock and muddy brown casts strongly points towards ischemic ATN.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Ischemic ATN:** Occurs due to prolonged renal hypoperfusion, leading to ATP depletion in tubular epithelial cells, particularly in the proximal tubules and thick ascending limb of the loop of Henle (which have high metabolic demands). This ATP depletion causes cell swelling, detachment from the basement membrane, and eventual necrosis. The necrotic cells slough off into the tubular lumen, forming the characteristic muddy brown casts (composed of cellular debris, myoglobin, and heme pigments). The process typically involves a "ischemic phase" (initial hypoperfusion) followed by a "toxic phase" (release of inflammatory mediators and toxins from damaged cells). The patient's hypotension and tachycardia indicate hypovolemic shock, a common cause of pre-renal azotemia that can progress to ischemic ATN if prolonged. The muddy brown casts are the key histological correlate of this process.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Acute interstitial nephritis (AIN) secondary to NSAID use:** AIN is characterized by inflammation of the renal interstitium, often involving eosinophils and lymphocytes. Common causes include drugs (NSAIDs, antibiotics, PPIs), infections, and autoimmune diseases. While NSAIDs can cause AKI (often pre-renal due to afferent arteriolar constriction or ATN), AIN typically presents with fever, rash, eosinophilia, and sterile pyuria/hematuria. Muddy brown casts are not characteristic of AIN; instead, white blood cell casts might be seen. The CT findings are also less typical for AIN, which often shows normal or slightly enlarged kidneys without significant perinephric stranding unless severe.
*   **(B) Contrast-induced nephropathy (CIN) following recent CT angiography:** CIN is a form of ATN caused by iodinated contrast agents. It typically occurs within 24-72 hours after contrast exposure. While it can cause AKI with muddy brown casts, the patient's presentation with severe hypotension and tachycardia suggests a different primary insult (shock) rather than contrast exposure as the sole cause. The vignette doesn't mention recent contrast exposure. Furthermore, CIN is often less severe than ATN from prolonged shock.
*   **(D) Glomerulonephritis associated with systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE):** SLE nephritis involves inflammation of the glomeruli. Clinical presentation often includes hematuria (microscopic or macroscopic), proteinuria, edema, hypertension, and potentially AKI. Urinalysis typically shows red blood cell casts, dysmorphic red blood cells, and proteinuria. Muddy brown casts are not characteristic of glomerulonephritis. The CT findings are also not specific for SLE nephritis. The patient's presentation lacks typical features of SLE (rash, joint pain, etc.) and the urinalysis findings are inconsistent.
*   **(E) Acute pyelonephritis complicated by renal abscess formation:** Acute pyelonephritis is a bacterial infection of the kidney parenchyma. It typically presents with fever, chills, flank pain, dysuria, and urinary frequency. Urinalysis shows pyuria, bacteriuria, and possibly white blood cell casts. A renal abscess is a localized collection of pus within the kidney. While pyelonephritis can cause flank pain and potentially AKI (sepsis-related), the urinalysis findings (muddy brown casts) and the CT findings (bilateral enlargement without specific abscess) are inconsistent with this diagnosis. The patient also lacks fever and other typical signs of infection.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature             | Ischemic ATN (Correct) | AIN (Distractor A) | CIN (Distractor B) | Glomerulonephritis (Distractor D) | Pyelonephritis (Distractor E) |
| :------------------ | :--------------------- | :----------------- | :----------------- | :-------------------------------- | :----------------------------- |
| **Cause**           | Prolonged Hypoperfusion | Drugs, Infection, Autoimmune | Iodinated Contrast | Immune Complex Deposition        | Bacterial Infection            |
| **Clinical Clues**  | Shock, Oliguria, Flank Pain | Fever, Rash, Eosinophilia | Recent Contrast Exposure | Hematuria, Proteinuria, Edema    | Fever, Chills, Dysuria         |
| **Urinalysis**      | Muddy Brown Casts      | WBCs, Eosinophils, +/- WBC Casts | Muddy Brown Casts (similar to ATN) | RBCs, RBC Casts, Proteinuria    | WBCs, Bacteria, WBC Casts      |
| **CT Findings**     | Bilateral Enlargement, Perinephric Stranding | Normal/Slightly Enlarged | Normal/Slightly Enlarged | Normal/Variable                | Focal/Diffuse Inflammation, Abscess |
| **Pathology**       | Tubular Cell Necrosis, Sloughing | Interstitial Inflammation | Tubular Cell Necrosis | Glomerular Inflammation         | Parenchymal Inflammation/Abscess |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Ischemic ATN is a common cause of AKI, often resulting from prolonged renal hypoperfusion (e.g., shock, severe dehydration). The hallmark urinalysis finding is muddy brown casts, representing sloughed necrotic tubular cells. Differentiating ATN from other causes of AKI like AIN, glomerulonephritis, and pyelonephritis relies on clinical context, urinalysis findings, and imaging. Remember that shock states are a major risk factor for ischemic ATN.

<a id='question-130'></a>

### Question 130: Renal Pathology: Focus 03-30 (• GI bleeding)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology of Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (HUS), specifically the role of Shiga toxin-producing *E. coli* (STEC) infection leading to microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (MAHA), thrombocytopenia, and acute kidney injury (AKI), characterized by thrombotic microangiopathy (TMA) in the renal glomeruli. (本题考察溶血尿毒综合征 (HUS) 的病理生理学，特别是产志贺毒素大肠杆菌 (STEC) 感染导致微血管病性溶血性贫血 (MAHA)、血小板减少症和急性肾损伤 (AKI)，其特征是肾小球内的血栓性微血管病 (TMA)。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of watery diarrhea followed by grossly bloody stools. His mother reports he has become increasingly lethargic and has decreased urine output over the past 24 hours. Vital signs show a temperature of 38.5°C, heart rate of 140 bpm, respiratory rate of 30 breaths/min, and blood pressure of 80/50 mmHg. Physical examination reveals pallor, petechiae on the trunk, and mild periorbital edema. Laboratory results show: Hemoglobin 7.5 g/dL (normal 11-14 g/dL), Platelets 40,000/µL (normal 150,000-400,000/µL), Serum Creatinine 3.5 mg/dL (normal 0.3-0.7 mg/dL), BUN 50 mg/dL (normal 7-20 mg/dL), LDH 1200 U/L (normal 100-220 U/L), Haptoglobin <10 mg/dL (normal 30-200 mg/dL), Peripheral blood smear shows schistocytes, and stool culture is positive for *E. coli* O157:H7. A renal biopsy is performed. Which of the following histological findings is most characteristic of the underlying renal pathology in this patient?

(A) Diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis with subepithelial humps.
(B) Crescent formation in Bowman's space with fibrinoid necrosis of glomerular capillaries.
(C) Mesangial IgA deposition with hypercellular glomeruli.
(D) Podocyte effacement with minimal changes in glomerular structure.
(E) Fibrinoid necrosis of arterioles with onion-skinning and luminal thrombosis.

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy - typical age group for STEC-HUS.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Watery diarrhea progressing to bloody stools (hemorrhagic colitis), lethargy, decreased urine output (oliguria).
- **Vital Signs:** Fever, tachycardia, tachypnea, hypotension (shock).
- **Physical Exam:** Pallor (anemia), petechiae (thrombocytopenia), periorbital edema (fluid overload/AKI).
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Anemia (Hb 7.5 g/dL)
    - Thrombocytopenia (Platelets 40,000/µL)
    - Acute Kidney Injury (Creatinine 3.5 mg/dL, BUN 50 mg/dL)
    - Evidence of Hemolysis (LDH 1200 U/L, Haptoglobin <10 mg/dL, Schistocytes on smear)
- **Stool Culture:** Positive for *E. coli* O157:H7 - confirms STEC infection.
- **Clinical Diagnosis:** The constellation of microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (MAHA), thrombocytopenia, and AKI following diarrheal illness caused by STEC strongly points to Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (HUS).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the characteristic histological finding in the kidney biopsy. HUS is a form of thrombotic microangiopathy (TMA).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Pathogenesis of STEC-HUS:** STEC (like *E. coli* O157:H7) produces Shiga toxin (Stx). Stx binds to the Gb3 receptor on renal endothelial cells (and other cells). Internalization leads to inhibition of protein synthesis via N-glycosidase activity, causing endothelial cell injury and activation.
- **Thrombotic Microangiopathy (TMA):** Endothelial damage leads to platelet activation and aggregation, formation of microthrombi within small vessels (arterioles and capillaries), particularly in the kidney.
- **Renal Manifestations:** Microthrombi obstruct blood flow, causing ischemia and damage to the glomeruli and tubules, leading to AKI.
- **Histopathology of HUS:** The hallmark finding is TMA in the renal microvasculature. This includes:
    - **Arteriolar Fibrinoid Necrosis:** Necrosis of the vessel wall with deposition of fibrin and plasma proteins.
    - **Onion-Skinning:** Concentric, laminated thickening of the arteriolar walls due to proliferation of endothelial and smooth muscle cells in response to injury.
    - **Luminal Thrombosis:** Presence of thrombi (platelets, fibrin, red blood cells) within the vessel lumen.
    - **Glomerular Changes:** Glomeruli may show mesangial widening, capillary thrombosis, and sometimes crescent formation (less common than in crescentic glomerulonephritis).
- **Other Features:** Schistocytes (fragmented red blood cells) are seen in the peripheral blood due to mechanical damage as RBCs pass through partially occluded microvessels.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis with subepithelial humps:** This describes post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis (PSGN). PSGN typically presents after a streptococcal infection (pharyngitis or skin infection) with hematuria, edema, and hypertension. The characteristic finding is immune complex deposition (IgG, C3) forming subepithelial "humps" on electron microscopy. This patient's presentation (diarrhea, MAHA, thrombocytopenia) is inconsistent with PSGN.
- (B) **Crescent formation in Bowman's space with fibrinoid necrosis of glomerular capillaries:** This describes rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis (RPGN), often associated with anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture syndrome), pauci-immune glomerulonephritis (ANCA-associated vasculitis), or immune complex glomerulonephritis. While fibrinoid necrosis can occur in HUS, prominent crescent formation is less typical than arteriolar changes. RPGN usually presents with rapidly declining renal function and severe hematuria. The primary pathology in HUS is TMA in arterioles, not primarily glomerular capillary necrosis leading to crescents.
- (C) **Mesangial IgA deposition with hypercellular glomeruli:** This describes IgA nephropathy (Berger's disease). It is the most common primary glomerulonephritis worldwide, often presenting with recurrent episodes of gross hematuria, especially after upper respiratory infections. The characteristic finding is IgA deposition in the mesangium on immunofluorescence. This patient's presentation is not typical for IgA nephropathy.
- (D) **Podocyte effacement with minimal changes in glomerular structure:** This describes minimal change disease, the most common cause of nephrotic syndrome in children. It presents with massive proteinuria, hypoalbuminemia, edema, and hyperlipidemia. Renal biopsy shows normal glomeruli on light microscopy and effacement (flattening) of podocyte foot processes on electron microscopy. This patient has AKI, MAHA, and thrombocytopenia, not nephrotic syndrome.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | HUS (STEC)                                  | PSGN                                     | RPGN (e.g., Anti-GBM)                     | IgA Nephropathy                          | Minimal Change Disease                  |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- |
| **Typical Presentation** | Diarrhea -> MAHA, Thrombocytopenia, AKI     | Pharyngitis/Skin Inf -> Hematuria, Edema | Rapidly declining renal function, Hematuria | Recurrent gross hematuria (URI trigger) | Nephrotic syndrome (proteinuria, edema) |
| **Age**                 | Often children                               | Children/Adults                           | Adults                                   | Young adults                             | Children                               |
| **Key Lab Findings**    | Schistocytes, Low Plt, High LDH, Low Haptoglobin, AKI | Hematuria, Low C3                      | Hematuria, Crescents on biopsy           | Hematuria, Normal C3, IgA deposition     | Massive proteinuria, Normal C3          |
| **Renal Biopsy**        | Arteriolar fibrinoid necrosis, Onion-skinning, Thrombosis | Subepithelial humps (EM)                | Crescent formation, Glomerular necrosis | Mesangial IgA deposition (IF)           | Podocyte effacement (EM)               |
| **Underlying Cause**    | Shiga toxin (STEC)                          | Group A Streptococcus                    | Anti-GBM Ab, ANCA, Immune complexes      | IgA deposition                           | Unknown (T-cell mediated?)              |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (HUS) is characterized by the triad of microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (MAHA), thrombocytopenia, and acute kidney injury (AKI), often following infection with Shiga toxin-producing *E. coli* (STEC). The renal pathology involves thrombotic microangiopathy (TMA), specifically fibrinoid necrosis and onion-skinning of arterioles, along with luminal thrombosis. Differentiating HUS from other causes of AKI and hematuria (like PSGN, RPGN, IgA nephropathy, minimal change disease) requires careful clinical correlation and understanding of their distinct pathological features.

<a id='question-131'></a>

### Question 131: Renal Pathology: Focus 03-31 (• Arthralgia)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology and clinical presentation of cryoglobulinemia, specifically mixed cryoglobulinemia type II (MCII) associated with Hepatitis C virus (HCV) infection, including its renal manifestations (membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis - MPGN) and systemic symptoms like arthralgia.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male with a history of chronic Hepatitis C virus (HCV) infection presents to the clinic complaining of worsening joint pain (arthralgia), fatigue, and purplish discoloration of his lower extremities (purpura). He denies recent infections or new medication use. Physical examination reveals palpable purpura on the lower legs, mild hepatosplenomegaly, and tender joints in his hands and knees. Laboratory tests show elevated serum creatinine (1.8 mg/dL, reference range 0.6-1.2 mg/dL), proteinuria (3+ on dipstick), and positive HCV RNA. A serum cryoglobulin test is positive. Renal biopsy shows membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN) with subendothelial immune complex deposits and microtubular architecture. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals granular deposits of IgG, IgM, and C3 along the glomerular capillary walls and mesangium.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism responsible for the patient's renal pathology?

(A) Deposition of circulating immune complexes containing rheumatoid factor, leading to complement activation and glomerular injury.
(B) Direct viral infection of podocytes by HCV, causing podocyte effacement and proteinuria.
(C) Formation of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies, resulting in linear IgG deposition and crescent formation.
(D) Immune complex deposition in the subepithelial space, characteristic of post-infectious glomerulonephritis.
(E) Antibody-mediated injury targeting the proximal renal tubules, leading to Fanconi syndrome.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male with chronic HCV infection. This immediately points towards potential HCV-related complications.
*   **Symptoms:** Arthralgia, fatigue, purpura (palpable purpura on lower legs). This triad is highly suggestive of mixed cryoglobulinemia (MC).
*   **Physical Exam:** Palpable purpura, hepatosplenomegaly, tender joints. Consistent with MC.
*   **Labs:** Elevated creatinine (renal dysfunction), proteinuria (glomerular damage), positive HCV RNA (etiology).
*   **Serum Cryoglobulin Test:** Positive. Confirms the presence of cryoglobulins.
*   **Renal Biopsy:** MPGN with subendothelial immune complex deposits and microtubular architecture. This is the classic renal manifestation of MCII.
*   **Immunofluorescence:** Granular IgG, IgM, and C3 deposits along capillary walls and mesangium. Confirms immune complex deposition.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying mechanism of the renal pathology.

The combination of chronic HCV, systemic symptoms (arthralgia, purpura), positive cryoglobulins, and MPGN on renal biopsy strongly indicates HCV-associated mixed cryoglobulinemia type II (MCII). MCII involves the production of immunoglobulins (typically IgM rheumatoid factor) that form immune complexes with HCV antigens. These complexes precipitate in the cold (cryoglobulins) and deposit in small vessels, including glomerular capillaries, leading to complement activation, inflammation, and MPGN.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **HCV and Cryoglobulinemia:** HCV infection, particularly genotype 1, is strongly associated with MCII. The virus triggers a chronic immune response.
*   **Mixed Cryoglobulinemia Type II (MCII):** Characterized by the presence of polyclonal IgM rheumatoid factor (RF) that binds to HCV antigens, forming mixed immune complexes. These complexes also contain IgG and complement components.
*   **Cryoprecipitation:** These immune complexes precipitate at low temperatures (typically <37°C), hence the term "cryoglobulin."
*   **Vascular Deposition:** The cryoprecipitated immune complexes deposit in small blood vessels, particularly in the skin (causing purpura), peripheral nerves (causing neuropathy), and kidneys (causing glomerulonephritis).
*   **Glomerular Injury:** In the kidney, these complexes deposit primarily in the subendothelial space and mesangium, leading to complement activation (C3 deposition), endothelial cell proliferation, mesangial cell proliferation, and thickening of the glomerular basement membrane. This results in the characteristic MPGN pattern with microtubular architecture.
*   **Mechanism:** The deposition of circulating immune complexes containing rheumatoid factor (IgM RF) complexed with HCV antigens is the fundamental mechanism driving the vasculitis and glomerulonephritis in MCII.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** This option accurately describes the mechanism of MCII associated with HCV. Circulating immune complexes containing IgM rheumatoid factor (RF) bind to HCV antigens, precipitate in the cold, deposit in glomeruli (subendothelial space), activate complement, and cause MPGN. This aligns perfectly with the clinical presentation and biopsy findings.
- (B): **Incorrect.** While HCV can infect various cells, direct viral infection of podocytes is not the primary mechanism for the glomerulonephritis seen in HCV-associated cryoglobulinemia. Podocyte injury can occur secondary to immune complex deposition and inflammation, but it's not the initiating event. The characteristic finding is MPGN due to immune complex deposition, not podocyte effacement typical of minimal change disease or FSGS.
- (C): **Incorrect.** Anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture syndrome) is characterized by antibodies targeting the glomerular basement membrane (specifically the alpha-3 chain of type IV collagen). This leads to linear IgG deposition on immunofluorescence, not granular deposits, and often causes rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis with crescent formation. The patient's immunofluorescence shows granular deposits, ruling out anti-GBM disease.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Post-infectious glomerulonephritis (PIGN) typically occurs after streptococcal or other infections. It is characterized by subepithelial immune complex deposits ("humps") on electron microscopy, often seen as a diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis. While it involves immune complexes, the location (subepithelial vs. subendothelial/mesangial) and the specific clinical context (HCV, cryoglobulins, purpura) are different.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Antibody-mediated injury targeting proximal tubules leads to Fanconi syndrome, characterized by impaired proximal tubular reabsorption (glucosuria, aminoaciduria, phosphaturia, bicarbonaturia). The patient's presentation (proteinuria, elevated creatinine, MPGN) points to glomerular injury, not tubular dysfunction.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | HCV-associated MCII (Correct) | Anti-GBM Disease | PIGN                  | Direct Viral Podocyte Injury | Proximal Tubule Injury (Fanconi) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :--------------- | :-------------------- | :---------------------------- | :------------------------------- |
| **Etiology**            | HCV infection                  | Autoimmune       | Post-infection        | Direct viral effect           | Autoimmune/Toxic                 |
| **Immune Complex**      | Yes (IgM RF + HCV Ag)          | No               | Yes (Subepithelial)   | No (Primary)                 | No                               |
| **Deposition Site**     | Subendothelial, Mesangial      | GBM (Linear IgG) | Subepithelial ("Humps") | N/A                           | N/A                              |
| **IF Pattern**          | Granular IgG, IgM, C3          | Linear IgG       | Granular IgG, C3      | N/A                           | N/A                              |
| **Histology**           | MPGN                           | Crescentic GN    | Diffuse Proliferative | Podocyte effacement           | Tubular atrophy/dilation         |
| **Systemic Symptoms**   | Purpura, Arthralgia, Neuropathy | Pulmonary Hemorrhage | Often absent          | Variable                      | Variable                         |
| **Key Lab Finding**     | Positive Cryoglobulins          | Anti-GBM Ab      | ASO titer             | N/A                           | Aminoaciduria, Glucosuria        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
HCV infection is a common cause of mixed cryoglobulinemia type II (MCII), leading to immune complex deposition (IgM RF + HCV Ag) in small vessels, including glomeruli. This results in MPGN characterized by subendothelial deposits and granular IgG/IgM/C3 staining. Key clinical features include purpura, arthralgia, and renal dysfunction. Differentiate MCII from other causes of glomerulonephritis like anti-GBM disease (linear IF), PIGN (subepithelial deposits), and direct viral injury (different histology).

<a id='question-132'></a>

### Question 132: Renal Pathology: Focus 03-32 (• Palpable purpura on legs and buttocks)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology and characteristic renal manifestation of Henoch-Schönlein Purpura (HSP), also known as IgA vasculitis, specifically focusing on the type of glomerular injury and the associated immune complex deposition. (本题考察Henoch-Schönlein Purpura (HSP), 又称IgA血管炎的病理生理学和特征性肾脏表现，特别是肾小球损伤类型和相关的免疫复合物沉积。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 7-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of a purpuric rash on his lower extremities and buttocks, joint pain in his knees and ankles, and abdominal pain. Physical examination reveals palpable purpura, predominantly on the extensor surfaces of the legs and buttocks. His blood pressure is 100/60 mmHg, heart rate is 110 bpm, and respiratory rate is 20 breaths/min. Laboratory tests show normal complete blood count, normal electrolytes, and elevated serum creatinine (1.8 mg/dL, reference range 0.5-1.2 mg/dL). Urinalysis reveals hematuria (microscopic) and proteinuria (2+). A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy shows diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis with mesangial hypercellularity and crescent formation in some glomeruli. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals granular deposits of IgA in the mesangium. Electron microscopy shows electron-dense deposits in the mesangium. Which of the following best describes the underlying pathological mechanism leading to the renal manifestations in this patient?

(A) Deposition of subepithelial immune complexes leading to membranous nephropathy.
(B) Formation of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies causing linear IgG deposition along the GBM.
(C) Deposition of IgA-containing immune complexes primarily in the mesangium, triggering complement activation and inflammation.
(D) Formation of circulating immune complexes containing rheumatoid factor, leading to membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN).
(E) Deposition of amyloid fibrils in the glomeruli, causing nephrotic syndrome.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 7-year-old boy - HSP is most common in children.
- **Symptoms:** Palpable purpura (legs, buttocks), joint pain (arthralgia/arthritis), abdominal pain - Classic tetrad of HSP.
- **Physical Exam:** Palpable purpura confirms vasculitis.
- **Labs:** Elevated creatinine (1.8 mg/dL) indicates acute kidney injury (AKI). Urinalysis shows hematuria and proteinuria, suggesting glomerulonephritis.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:**
    - Light Microscopy: Diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis, mesangial hypercellularity, crescent formation - Indicates severe glomerular inflammation and injury.
    - Immunofluorescence: Granular IgA deposits in the mesangium - Pathognomonic for IgA vasculitis (HSP).
    - Electron Microscopy: Electron-dense deposits in the mesangium - Corroborates the immunofluorescence findings.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying pathological mechanism.
- **Connecting Clues:** The clinical presentation (palpable purpura, arthritis, abdominal pain in a child) strongly suggests HSP. The renal biopsy findings (mesangial IgA deposition, proliferative GN with crescents) confirm the diagnosis of IgA vasculitis nephritis. The question asks for the mechanism linking the IgA deposition to the renal damage. IgA deposition in the mesangium triggers complement activation (alternative pathway primarily) and inflammatory cell infiltration, leading to glomerular injury, hematuria, proteinuria, and potentially AKI (as seen with elevated creatinine and crescent formation).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **HSP Pathogenesis:** HSP (IgA vasculitis) is a systemic small-vessel vasculitis characterized by the deposition of IgA-containing immune complexes in various tissues, including the skin, joints, gastrointestinal tract, and kidneys.
- **Renal Involvement:** The kidney is a frequent target organ. The hallmark of HSP nephritis is the deposition of IgA immune complexes predominantly within the glomerular mesangium.
- **Mechanism of Injury:** The deposition of these IgA complexes activates the alternative pathway of complement, leading to the generation of C3a and C5a (anaphylatoxins) and the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC). This triggers inflammation, attracting neutrophils and macrophages to the glomeruli. These inflammatory cells release proteases, reactive oxygen species, and cytokines, causing damage to the glomerular structure, particularly the mesangial cells and podocytes. This results in mesangial proliferation, endocapillary hypercellularity, and potentially crescent formation in severe cases, leading to hematuria, proteinuria, and impaired glomerular filtration (AKI).
- **Biopsy Correlation:** The biopsy findings directly reflect this mechanism: IgA deposition (IF), mesangial hypercellularity and proliferation (LM), and electron-dense deposits (EM). Crescent formation indicates severe injury and rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Deposition of subepithelial immune complexes is characteristic of membranous nephropathy, typically associated with IgG and C3 deposition. This pattern is not seen in HSP. Membranous nephropathy usually presents with nephrotic syndrome, not the acute nephritic syndrome with crescents seen here.
- (B): Anti-GBM antibodies cause Goodpasture syndrome or anti-GBM disease. Immunofluorescence shows linear IgG deposition along the glomerular basement membrane, not granular mesangial IgA deposits. This condition typically presents with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and often pulmonary hemorrhage.
- (D): Circulating immune complexes containing rheumatoid factor are associated with cryoglobulinemia, which can cause MPGN. MPGN typically shows subendothelial and/or mesangial deposits, often with a "tram-track" appearance of the GBM on light microscopy. While MPGN can present with nephritic features, the specific IgA deposition in the mesangium is diagnostic for HSP nephritis.
- (E): Amyloid deposition causes amyloidosis, which can affect the kidneys leading to nephrotic syndrome (often with proteinuria and fatty casts) or, less commonly, nephritic syndrome. Renal biopsy shows amorphous, eosinophilic deposits in the glomeruli and interstitium that stain positive with Congo red and show apple-green birefringence under polarized light. This is distinct from the IgA deposits seen in HSP.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | HSP Nephritis (IgA Vasculitis) | Membranous Nephropathy | Anti-GBM Disease | MPGN (Cryoglobulinemia) | Amyloidosis |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :--------------------- | :--------------- | :---------------------- | :---------- |
| **Age Group**           | Primarily Children             | Adults                 | Adults           | Adults                  | Older Adults |
| **Clinical Presentation** | Purpura, Arthritis, Abdominal Pain, Nephritis | Nephrotic Syndrome   | RPGN, +/- Hemoptysis | Nephritic/Nephrotic    | Nephrotic/Renal Failure |
| **Immune Deposits**     | Granular IgA in Mesangium      | Subepithelial IgG/C3   | Linear IgG along GBM | Subendothelial/Mesangial IgG/C3/RF | Amyloid (Congo Red+) |
| **Light Microscopy**     | Mesangial Hypercellularity, Proliferative GN, Crescents | Thickened GBM, Spikes | Crescentic GN           | Tram-track GBM, Hypercellularity | Amorphous Deposits |
| **Electron Microscopy**  | Electron-dense deposits in Mesangium | Subepithelial Deposits | None (GBM damage) | Subendothelial/Mesangial Deposits | Fibrillar Deposits |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
HSP nephritis is characterized by IgA deposition in the glomerular mesangium, leading to mesangial proliferation and inflammation. Key differentials include other causes of glomerulonephritis like post-infectious GN (often post-streptococcal), membranoproliferative GN, anti-GBM disease, and lupus nephritis. Always correlate clinical presentation (especially palpable purpura in children) with biopsy findings. Remember IgA deposition in the mesangium is the hallmark of HSP nephritis.
