<a id='question-142'></a>

### Question 142: Renal Pathology: Focus 03-42 (• Renal biopsy. Light microscopy may show normal g)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of minimal change disease (MCD), a common cause of nephrotic syndrome in children, and its characteristic findings on renal biopsy, specifically the normal appearance on light microscopy and the diagnostic finding on electron microscopy (EM). It also assesses the ability to differentiate MCD from other causes of nephrotic syndrome with different histological features.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy presents to the pediatrician with periorbital edema, generalized swelling, and decreased urine output over the past week. His parents report he seemed healthy until recently. Physical examination reveals significant edema, hypertension (110/70 mmHg), and 3+ pitting edema in the lower extremities. Urinalysis shows 4+ proteinuria and no hematuria. Serum albumin is low (2.1 g/dL, normal range 3.5-5.0 g/dL), and serum creatinine is normal (0.5 mg/dL, normal range 0.3-0.7 mg/dL). A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy of the glomeruli shows normal morphology. Immunofluorescence microscopy is negative for IgG, IgA, IgM, C3, and C1q deposits. Electron microscopy reveals diffuse effacement (flattening) of podocyte foot processes. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Focal Segmental Glomerulosclerosis (FSGS)
(B) Membranous Nephropathy
(C) Minimal Change Disease (MCD)
(D) IgA Nephropathy
(E) Post-infectious Glomerulonephritis

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 6-year-old boy. This age group is highly susceptible to Minimal Change Disease (MCD), the most common cause of nephrotic syndrome in children.
*   **Presenting Symptoms:** Periorbital edema, generalized swelling, decreased urine output. These are classic signs of nephrotic syndrome due to fluid retention from hypoalbuminemia.
*   **Physical Exam:** Significant edema, hypertension, pitting edema. Consistent with nephrotic syndrome and associated fluid overload.
*   **Urinalysis:** 4+ proteinuria, no hematuria. Heavy proteinuria (>3.5 g/day or equivalent) is the hallmark of nephrotic syndrome. The absence of significant hematuria points away from nephritic syndromes.
*   **Serum Labs:** Low albumin (2.1 g/dL), normal creatinine (0.5 mg/dL). Hypoalbuminemia is characteristic of nephrotic syndrome. Normal creatinine indicates preserved renal function at this stage.
*   **Renal Biopsy Findings:**
    *   **Light Microscopy (LM):** Normal glomeruli. This is the defining feature of Minimal Change Disease. Other causes of nephrotic syndrome typically show abnormalities on LM (e.g., segmental sclerosis in FSGS, thickened basement membranes in Membranous Nephropathy, mesangial proliferation in IgA Nephropathy).
    *   **Immunofluorescence (IF):** Negative for all immunoglobulins and complement components. This rules out immune complex-mediated glomerular diseases like Membranous Nephropathy, IgA Nephropathy, and Lupus Nephritis.
    *   **Electron Microscopy (EM):** Diffuse effacement (flattening) of podocyte foot processes. This is the pathognomonic finding for MCD, representing podocyte injury leading to proteinuria.

*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely diagnosis based on the clinical presentation and the specific biopsy findings (normal LM, negative IF, podocyte effacement on EM).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Minimal Change Disease (MCD):** An idiopathic disorder characterized by podocyte injury, leading to loss of the glomerular filtration barrier's negative charge and increased permeability to proteins, primarily albumin.
*   **Pathogenesis:** Thought to be T-cell mediated, releasing cytokines (like IL-13) that damage podocytes and cause foot process effacement. Genetic factors may also play a role.
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Abrupt onset of nephrotic syndrome (heavy proteinuria, hypoalbuminemia, edema, hyperlipidemia) in children. Usually responds well to corticosteroids.
*   **Biopsy Findings:**
    *   **LM:** Glomeruli appear normal.
    *   **IF:** Negative for immune deposits.
    *   **EM:** Diffuse effacement of podocyte foot processes. This is the diagnostic hallmark.
*   **Differential Diagnosis:** The key is differentiating MCD from other causes of nephrotic syndrome based on biopsy findings. FSGS shows segmental sclerosis on LM. Membranous Nephropathy shows thickened glomerular basement membranes on LM and granular deposits on IF. IgA Nephropathy shows mesangial IgA deposits on IF. Post-infectious GN typically shows subepithelial "humps" on EM and immune complex deposits on IF.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Focal Segmental Glomerulosclerosis (FSGS):** This condition also causes nephrotic syndrome, but light microscopy would show *segmental* sclerosis (scarring) in *some* glomeruli (focal). EM shows foot process effacement, but LM findings are distinct from the normal LM seen in MCD.
- (B) **Membranous Nephropathy:** This is a common cause of nephrotic syndrome in adults but can occur in children. Light microscopy shows thickened glomerular basement membranes (GBM), and immunofluorescence shows granular deposits of IgG and C3 along the GBM. EM shows subepithelial immune deposits. The LM and IF findings are inconsistent with the vignette.
- (D) **IgA Nephropathy:** This is the most common glomerulonephritis worldwide, often presenting with hematuria (nephritic syndrome) but can sometimes cause nephrotic syndrome. Light microscopy shows mesangial proliferation. Immunofluorescence shows characteristic mesangial IgA deposits. The LM and IF findings are inconsistent with the vignette.
- (E) **Post-infectious Glomerulonephritis:** Typically presents as nephritic syndrome (hematuria, hypertension, edema, oliguria) after a streptococcal infection, although nephrotic-range proteinuria can occur. Light microscopy shows diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis. Immunofluorescence shows granular deposits of IgG and C3. EM shows characteristic subepithelial "humps". The clinical presentation (primarily nephrotic) and biopsy findings (normal LM, negative IF) are inconsistent with the vignette.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Minimal Change Disease (MCD) | FSGS                     | Membranous Nephropathy   | IgA Nephropathy          | Post-infectious GN      |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------- |
| **Typical Age**         | Children (2-6 yrs)           | Variable (Adults > Kids) | Adults                  | Young Adults             | Children/Adults          |
| **Clinical Syndrome**   | Nephrotic Syndrome           | Nephrotic Syndrome       | Nephrotic Syndrome       | Nephritic/Nephrotic      | Nephritic (often)        |
| **Light Microscopy (LM)** | Normal Glomeruli             | Segmental Sclerosis      | Thickened GBM           | Mesangial Proliferation  | Diffuse Proliferation    |
| **Immunofluorescence (IF)** | Negative                    | Variable (often negative) | Granular IgG, C3 (GBM)   | Mesangial IgA            | Granular IgG, C3         |
| **Electron Microscopy (EM)** | Diffuse Foot Process Effacement | Foot Process Effacement | Subepithelial Deposits | Mesangial Deposits      | Subepithelial "Humps"    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Minimal Change Disease is characterized by nephrotic syndrome with normal glomeruli on light microscopy and diffuse podocyte foot process effacement on electron microscopy. It is the most common cause of nephrotic syndrome in children. Differentiating it from other glomerular diseases relies heavily on the specific findings on renal biopsy. Key differentials include FSGS (segmental sclerosis on LM), Membranous Nephropathy (thickened GBM and granular IgG/C3 on LM/IF), and IgA Nephropathy (mesangial IgA on IF).

<a id='question-143'></a>

### Question 143: Renal Pathology: IgA Nephropathy
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of IgA nephropathy (Berger's disease), specifically its characteristic immunofluorescence findings, clinical presentation, and associated pathology. (本题考察IgA肾病（伯杰病）的理解，特别是其特征性免疫荧光发现、临床表现和相关病理学。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old male presents to the clinic complaining of recurrent episodes of gross hematuria, typically occurring 1-2 days after an upper respiratory tract infection. He reports that the episodes have been happening for the past 5 years. He denies flank pain, fever, or significant proteinuria. His past medical history is unremarkable, and he takes no medications. Physical examination reveals normal vital signs and no edema. Urinalysis during an episode of gross hematuria shows microscopic hematuria with red blood cell casts. A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy reveals mesangial hypercellularity and endocapillary proliferation. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows granular deposits of IgA in the mesangium. Electron microscopy shows electron-dense deposits in the mesangium. Which of the following findings is most characteristic of this patient's condition?

(A) Subepithelial immune complex deposits ("humps") on light microscopy, with C3 deposition on immunofluorescence.
(B) Linear deposition of IgG along the glomerular basement membrane on immunofluorescence.
(C) Mesangial IgA deposition on immunofluorescence, often associated with synovial IgA deposition.
(D) Pauci-immune glomerulonephritis with crescent formation and absence of significant immune deposits on immunofluorescence.
(E) Subendothelial immune complex deposits with "wire loop" lesions on light microscopy and IgG/C3 deposition on immunofluorescence.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 28-year-old male. Young adult presentation is common for IgA nephropathy.
*   **Chief Complaint:** Recurrent gross hematuria following upper respiratory tract infections (synpharyngitic hematuria). This is the classic presentation of IgA nephropathy.
*   **Duration:** 5 years of recurrent episodes. Chronic, relapsing course is typical.
*   **Associated Symptoms:** Absence of flank pain, fever, or significant proteinuria suggests a less severe form, although proteinuria can occur.
*   **Urinalysis:** Microscopic hematuria with red blood cell casts during an episode. RBC casts indicate glomerular origin of hematuria.
*   **Renal Biopsy Findings:**
    *   Light Microscopy: Mesangial hypercellularity and endocapillary proliferation. These are common findings in IgA nephropathy.
    *   Immunofluorescence: Granular deposits of IgA in the mesangium. This is the pathognomonic finding for IgA nephropathy.
    *   Electron Microscopy: Electron-dense deposits in the mesangium. Consistent with immune complex deposition.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most characteristic* finding of this condition based on the clinical and pathological information provided.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **IgA Nephropathy Pathogenesis:** IgA nephropathy is characterized by the deposition of IgA-containing immune complexes in the glomerular mesangium. The exact trigger is unknown, but it is often associated with mucosal infections (especially respiratory or gastrointestinal) leading to increased production of IgA antibodies. These IgA complexes deposit in the mesangium, activate complement, and cause inflammation, leading to glomerular damage.
*   **Characteristic Findings:** The hallmark finding is mesangial IgA deposition detected by immunofluorescence microscopy. This is often accompanied by C3 deposition and mesangial cell proliferation on light microscopy. Electron microscopy shows electron-dense deposits within the mesangium.
*   **Clinical Correlation:** The synpharyngitic hematuria (hematuria occurring shortly after an infection) is highly suggestive of IgA nephropathy.
*   **Extrarenal Manifestations:** IgA nephropathy can be associated with IgA deposition in other mucosal sites, such as the joints (synovitis), skin (dermatitis herpetiformis-like lesions), and gastrointestinal tract.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Subepithelial immune complex deposits ("humps") and C3 deposition are characteristic of post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis (PSGN). PSGN typically presents acutely after a streptococcal infection, often with edema and hypertension, and the deposits are primarily C3 and IgG, located subepithelially. This patient has a chronic, relapsing course linked to URI, not strep throat, and the biopsy shows IgA, not C3 humps.
- (B): Linear deposition of IgG along the glomerular basement membrane (GBM) is characteristic of anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture syndrome). This condition typically presents with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and often pulmonary hemorrhage. The immunofluorescence pattern is distinctly linear, unlike the granular mesangial pattern seen in IgA nephropathy.
- (D): Pauci-immune glomerulonephritis (minimal or no immune deposits on immunofluorescence) with crescent formation is characteristic of ANCA-associated vasculitis (e.g., granulomatosis with polyangiitis, microscopic polyangiitis). These conditions often present with systemic symptoms and rapidly progressive renal failure. The lack of significant immune deposits distinguishes them from IgA nephropathy.
- (E): Subendothelial immune complex deposits with "wire loop" lesions and IgG/C3 deposition are characteristic of lupus nephritis (specifically Class IV, diffuse proliferative lupus nephritis). Lupus nephritis is associated with systemic lupus erythematosus and typically involves other organ systems. The deposits are subendothelial, and the immunofluorescence shows IgG and C3, not predominantly IgA in the mesangium.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                     | IgA Nephropathy (Berger's) | Post-Streptococcal GN | Anti-GBM Disease | ANCA-Associated GN | Lupus Nephritis (Class IV) |
| :-------------------------- | :------------------------- | :-------------------- | :--------------- | :----------------- | :-------------------------- |
| **Clinical Presentation**   | Synpharyngitic hematuria, recurrent | Acute, post-strep infection | RPGN, pulm hemorrhage | RPGN, systemic symptoms | Systemic lupus symptoms    |
| **Light Microscopy**        | Mesangial hypercellularity, endocapillary proliferation | Hypercellularity, "humps" | Crescent formation | Crescent formation, pauci-immune | Diffuse proliferation, "wire loops" |
| **Immunofluorescence**      | Granular IgA in mesangium   | Granular C3, IgG (subepithelial) | Linear IgG along GBM | Pauci-immune        | Granular IgG, C3 (subendothelial) |
| **Electron Microscopy**     | Electron-dense deposits in mesangium | Subepithelial humps | Linear deposits along GBM | N/A                  | Subendothelial deposits    |
| **Associated Conditions**   | Synovial IgA deposition    | Recent strep infection | Goodpasture's   | ANCA vasculitis    | Systemic Lupus Erythematosus |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
IgA nephropathy is the most common primary glomerulonephritis worldwide, characterized by mesangial IgA deposition. The classic presentation is recurrent gross hematuria following mucosal infections. Differentiating it from other glomerulonephritides relies heavily on the specific immunofluorescence pattern and clinical context. Remember to distinguish IgA nephropathy (mesangial IgA) from PSGN (subepithelial C3 humps), Anti-GBM disease (linear IgG), ANCA-associated GN (pauci-immune), and Lupus nephritis (subendothelial IgG/C3).

<a id='question-144'></a>

### Question 144: Renal Pathology: Focus 03-44 (C3. Electron microscopy shows mesangial immune com)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the characteristic electron microscopy (EM) findings in IgA nephropathy, specifically the presence of mesangial immune complex deposits, and its correlation with clinical presentation and other diagnostic findings. (本题考察IgA肾病中电子显微镜下特征性的系膜基质内免疫复合物沉积，以及其与临床表现和其他诊断发现的相关性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the clinic with recurrent episodes of gross hematuria, typically occurring 1-2 days after an upper respiratory tract infection. He denies flank pain, fever, or significant proteinuria. His past medical history is unremarkable, and he takes no medications. Physical examination reveals normal vital signs and no edema. Urinalysis shows microscopic hematuria and mild proteinuria (1+). Serum creatinine is 1.1 mg/dL (normal range 0.7-1.3 mg/dL). A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy shows mesangial hypercellularity and endocapillary proliferation. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals granular deposits of IgA in the mesangium. Electron microscopy shows electron-dense deposits localized primarily within the mesangium.

Which of the following findings on electron microscopy is most characteristic of this patient's condition?
(A) Subepithelial electron-dense deposits ("humps")
(B) Subendothelial electron-dense deposits
(C) Linear deposits along the glomerular basement membrane (GBM)
(D) Mesangial electron-dense deposits
(E) Podocyte effacement

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male.
- **Chief Complaint:** Recurrent gross hematuria following upper respiratory infections (synpharyngitic hematuria). This is a classic presentation for IgA nephropathy.
- **Symptoms:** No flank pain, fever (rules out acute pyelonephritis), or significant proteinuria (suggests less severe glomerular damage initially).
- **Physical Exam:** Normal vital signs, no edema (consistent with relatively preserved renal function and minimal proteinuria).
- **Labs:** Microscopic hematuria, mild proteinuria (1+), normal serum creatinine (1.1 mg/dL). These findings are consistent with glomerular inflammation causing hematuria and mild proteinuria, but without significant impairment of GFR.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:**
    - **Light Microscopy:** Mesangial hypercellularity and endocapillary proliferation. These are common findings in various forms of glomerulonephritis, including IgA nephropathy.
    - **Immunofluorescence:** Granular deposits of IgA in the mesangium. This is the hallmark diagnostic finding for IgA nephropathy.
    - **Electron Microscopy:** The question asks for the characteristic EM finding. The vignette states electron-dense deposits localized primarily within the mesangium. This directly corresponds to option (D).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **IgA Nephropathy Pathogenesis:** IgA nephropathy is characterized by the deposition of IgA-containing immune complexes in the glomerular mesangium. This deposition triggers inflammation, leading to glomerular damage.
- **Electron Microscopy in IgA Nephropathy:** EM reveals electron-dense deposits within the mesangial matrix. These deposits represent the accumulated IgA immune complexes. While deposits can sometimes extend to the subendothelial space, the primary and most characteristic location is the mesangium.
- **Differential Diagnosis & EM Findings:**
    - **Post-streptococcal Glomerulonephritis (PSGN):** Characterized by subepithelial "humps" on EM.
    - **Membranous Nephropathy:** Characterized by subepithelial deposits along the GBM.
    - **Anti-GBM Disease (Goodpasture Syndrome):** Characterized by linear deposits along the GBM on immunofluorescence and EM.
    - **Minimal Change Disease:** Normal LM and IF, podocyte effacement on EM.
    - **Focal Segmental Glomerulosclerosis (FSGS):** Podocyte effacement on EM.
    - **Lupus Nephritis:** Various patterns depending on class, including mesangial, subendothelial, and subepithelial deposits.
- **Correlation:** The clinical presentation (synpharyngitic hematuria), IF findings (mesangial IgA), and LM findings (mesangial hypercellularity) strongly point towards IgA nephropathy. The EM finding described (mesangial electron-dense deposits) is the characteristic ultrastructural correlate of this diagnosis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Subepithelial electron-dense deposits ("humps"):** This finding is characteristic of post-infectious glomerulonephritis, particularly post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis (PSGN). PSGN typically presents with hematuria, edema, and hypertension, often following a streptococcal infection, and the EM shows distinct subepithelial humps. This patient's presentation (recurrent hematuria after URI, no edema) and IF findings (IgA, not IgG/C3) are inconsistent with PSGN.
- (B) **Subendothelial electron-dense deposits:** These deposits are seen in conditions like lupus nephritis (especially Class III and IV) and membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN). While IgA deposits can sometimes extend subendothelially in IgA nephropathy, the primary and most characteristic location is mesangial. The clinical picture and IF findings are more typical for IgA nephropathy than lupus nephritis or MPGN.
- (C) **Linear deposits along the glomerular basement membrane (GBM):** This finding on immunofluorescence (and EM) is pathognomonic for anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture syndrome). This condition typically presents with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and often pulmonary hemorrhage. The patient's presentation and IF findings (granular mesangial IgA) are completely different.
- (E) **Podocyte effacement:** This finding, characterized by the flattening and fusion of podocyte foot processes, is seen on EM in minimal change disease, focal segmental glomerulosclerosis (FSGS), and membranous nephropathy. While podocyte injury can occur in IgA nephropathy, diffuse effacement is not the primary or characteristic EM finding. The hallmark is the mesangial deposits.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                     | IgA Nephropathy                                  | PSGN                                       | Membranous Nephropathy                      | Anti-GBM Disease                           | Minimal Change Disease                     |
| :-------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :----------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- |
| **Clinical Presentation**   | Synpharyngitic hematuria, recurrent hematuria   | Hematuria, edema, HTN post-infection        | Nephrotic syndrome                          | RPGN, pulmonary hemorrhage                 | Nephrotic syndrome (children)              |
| **Light Microscopy**        | Mesangial hypercellularity, endocapillary prolif | Hypercellularity, endocapillary prolif     | Thickened GBM, normal/mild hypercellularity | Crescent formation, hypercellularity       | Normal glomeruli                          |
| **Immunofluorescence**      | Granular mesangial IgA                          | Granular IgG, C3 (starry sky)              | Granular IgG, C3 along GBM                  | Linear IgG along GBM                       | Negative                                  |
| **Electron Microscopy**     | Mesangial electron-dense deposits               | Subepithelial "humps"                      | Subepithelial deposits along GBM            | Linear deposits along GBM                  | Podocyte effacement                       |
| **Key Diagnostic Feature** | Mesangial IgA deposits                          | Subepithelial humps                         | Subepithelial deposits                      | Linear IgG deposits                        | Podocyte effacement                       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
IgA nephropathy is the most common primary glomerulonephritis worldwide, often presenting with recurrent gross hematuria following mucosal infections. The diagnostic hallmark is granular mesangial IgA deposition on immunofluorescence, correlating with mesangial electron-dense deposits on electron microscopy. Differentiating IgA nephropathy from other glomerulonephritides relies on recognizing these characteristic findings, especially the location of immune deposits on EM (mesangial vs. subepithelial/subendothelial) and IF (IgA vs. IgG/C3 vs. linear IgG).

<a id='question-145'></a>

### Question 145: Renal Pathology: Focus 03-45 (• Many cases slowly progress to renal failure over)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the pathological features and clinical progression of IgA nephropathy, specifically focusing on the characteristic mesangial IgA deposition and its association with crescent formation and eventual renal failure. (IgA肾病，重点考点：网状细胞内IgA沉积的特征性病理学表现及其与新月体形成和最终肾功能衰竭的关联。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the clinic complaining of intermittent gross hematuria, particularly after upper respiratory tract infections, for the past 5 years. He denies flank pain, fever, or dysuria. His past medical history is unremarkable, and he takes no medications. Physical examination reveals mild hypertension (145/90 mmHg) and no edema. Urinalysis shows microscopic hematuria (50 RBCs/hpf) and mild proteinuria (1+). Serum creatinine is 1.2 mg/dL (reference range 0.7-1.3 mg/dL), and estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) is 75 mL/min/1.73 m². A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy shows diffuse mesangial proliferation and hypercellularity. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals granular deposits of IgA in the mesangium. Electron microscopy shows electron-dense deposits in the mesangium. Over the next 10 years, the patient's proteinuria increases to nephrotic range, his hypertension worsens, and his serum creatinine rises progressively to 4.0 mg/dL, requiring initiation of hemodialysis. Which of the following pathological findings is most characteristic of the progression of this patient's disease to end-stage renal disease?

(A) Linear deposition of IgG along the glomerular basement membrane.
(B) Formation of large, cellular crescent structures in Bowman's space.
(C) Deposition of amyloid fibrils in the glomeruli and interstitium.
(D) Podocyte effacement and subepithelial humps.
(E) Granular deposits of C3 in the subendothelial space.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male.
- **Presenting Complaint:** Intermittent gross hematuria, often following URIs, for 5 years. This is a classic presentation for IgA nephropathy (Berger's disease).
- **Symptoms:** Absence of flank pain, fever, dysuria suggests against acute infection or nephrolithiasis.
- **Physical Exam:** Mild hypertension (145/90 mmHg). Hypertension is a common complication of chronic kidney disease.
- **Urinalysis:** Microscopic hematuria (50 RBCs/hpf) and mild proteinuria (1+). Consistent with glomerular disease.
- **Labs:** Serum creatinine 1.2 mg/dL, eGFR 75 mL/min/1.73 m². Indicates mild chronic kidney disease (CKD) at presentation.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:**
    - Light Microscopy: Diffuse mesangial proliferation and hypercellularity.
    - Immunofluorescence: Granular deposits of IgA in the mesangium. This is the hallmark diagnostic feature of IgA nephropathy.
    - Electron Microscopy: Electron-dense deposits in the mesangium. Confirms the presence of immune complex deposition.
- **Clinical Course:** Over 10 years, proteinuria increases to nephrotic range, hypertension worsens, serum creatinine rises to 4.0 mg/dL, requiring hemodialysis. This indicates progressive chronic kidney disease leading to end-stage renal disease (ESRD).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the pathological finding most characteristic of the *progression* of this patient's disease (IgA nephropathy) to ESRD.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **IgA Nephropathy Pathogenesis:** IgA nephropathy is characterized by the deposition of IgA-containing immune complexes in the glomerular mesangium. The exact trigger is unknown, but it's thought to involve aberrant glycosylation of IgA1, leading to its deposition in the kidney, particularly after mucosal infections (like URIs).
- **Initial Pathology:** The initial biopsy findings (mesangial hypercellularity, IgA deposits) are characteristic.
- **Progression to ESRD:** While many patients have a benign course, a significant proportion (around 20-40%) progress to chronic kidney disease and ESRD over 10-20 years. The mechanisms of progression are complex but involve:
    - **Mesangial Expansion:** Persistent inflammation and matrix deposition lead to mesangial expansion, scarring, and glomerulosclerosis.
    - **Crescent Formation:** In some cases, severe inflammation can lead to the formation of crescents (proliferation of parietal epithelial cells and infiltration of inflammatory cells into Bowman's space), which rapidly destroy the glomerular structure. Crescentic IgA nephropathy is associated with a more aggressive course and faster progression to ESRD.
    - **Tubulointerstitial Fibrosis:** Chronic inflammation and injury also affect the tubules and interstitium, contributing to fibrosis and loss of renal function.
- **End-Stage Renal Disease Pathology:** The final common pathway for ESRD in many glomerular diseases, including progressive IgA nephropathy, involves widespread glomerulosclerosis, tubulointerstitial fibrosis, and vascular changes. However, the *characteristic* feature often associated with *rapid* progression or severe forms, which can lead to ESRD, is crescent formation. While not *all* cases progressing to ESRD show crescents, it is a significant pathological finding associated with a more severe and rapidly progressive course of IgA nephropathy.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Linear deposition of IgG along the glomerular basement membrane:** This is characteristic of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) disease (Goodpasture syndrome), not IgA nephropathy. IgA nephropathy shows granular mesangial deposits.
- (C) **Deposition of amyloid fibrils in the glomeruli and interstitium:** This describes amyloidosis, a distinct condition characterized by the deposition of misfolded proteins (amyloid fibrils), not immune complexes like IgA.
- (D) **Podocyte effacement and subepithelial humps:** Podocyte effacement is seen in many proteinuric kidney diseases, including minimal change disease, FSGS, and membranous nephropathy. Subepithelial humps are characteristic of post-infectious glomerulonephritis (typically post-streptococcal), which involves immune complex deposition but not primarily IgA in the mesangium.
- (E) **Granular deposits of C3 in the subendothelial space:** Granular subendothelial C3 deposits are characteristic of membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN), particularly type I (immune complex-mediated) or type II (dense deposit disease/C3 glomerulopathy). While C3 can be present in IgA nephropathy, the primary and diagnostic deposit is IgA in the mesangium, and the location is typically mesangial, not subendothelial.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                     | IgA Nephropathy (Berger's) | Anti-GBM Disease | Amyloidosis        | Post-infectious GN | MPGN Type I      |
| :-------------------------- | :------------------------- | :--------------- | :---------------- | :----------------- | :--------------- |
| **Primary Deposit**         | IgA (mesangial)            | IgG (GBM)        | Amyloid fibrils   | Immune complexes   | Immune complexes |
| **Deposit Location**        | Mesangium                  | GBM (linear)     | Glomeruli/Interstitium | Subepithelial (humps) | Subendothelial   |
| **Light Microscopy**        | Mesangial hypercellularity | Crescentic GN    | Amorphous deposits | Diffuse proliferation | Mesangial proliferation, GBM thickening |
| **Immunofluorescence**      | Granular IgA (mesangium)    | Linear IgG (GBM) | Congo red (+)     | Granular IgG/C3    | Granular IgG/C3/C1q |
| **Electron Microscopy**     | Electron-dense deposits (mesangium) | Linear deposits (GBM) | Fibrils           | Subepithelial humps | Subendothelial deposits |
| **Clinical Presentation**   | Recurrent hematuria (URI+) | Rapidly progressive GN | Proteinuria, nephrotic syndrome | Hematuria, edema, HTN (post-infection) | Hematuria, proteinuria, HTN |
| **Progression to ESRD**     | Slow (20-40% over decades) | Rapid            | Variable          | Usually resolves   | Variable          |
| **Crescent Formation**      | Possible (severe cases)    | Common           | Rare              | Rare               | Possible          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
IgA nephropathy is the most common primary glomerulonephritis worldwide, characterized by IgA deposition in the mesangium. While many cases are benign, a subset progresses to ESRD, often associated with crescent formation. Differentiating IgA nephropathy from other causes of hematuria and proteinuria requires careful consideration of clinical presentation and biopsy findings. Remember the hallmark is *granular IgA in the mesangium*.

<a id='question-146'></a>

### Question 146: Renal Pathology: Membranoproliferative Glomerulonephritis (MPGN)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN) is characterized by glomerular basement membrane (GBM) thickening, mesangial hypercellularity, and endocapillary proliferation, often associated with complement pathway dysregulation (e.g., alternative pathway activation leading to C3 deposition). (膜性增生性肾小球肾炎 (MPGN) 的特征是肾小球基底膜 (GBM) 增厚、网状细胞增生和内毛细血管增生，通常与补体途径失调有关（例如，替代途径激活导致 C3 沉积）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old male presents to the clinic complaining of insidious onset of lower extremity edema and dark urine over the past 3 months. He denies recent infections, fever, or joint pain. His past medical history is unremarkable except for occasional upper respiratory infections during childhood. Physical examination reveals bilateral pitting edema up to the knees and blood pressure of 150/95 mmHg. Urinalysis shows 3+ proteinuria, microscopic hematuria (20 RBCs/hpf), and no casts. Serum creatinine is 1.8 mg/dL (reference range 0.6-1.2 mg/dL), and serum albumin is 2.5 g/dL (reference range 3.5-5.0 g/dL). A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy shows hypercellular glomeruli with thickened capillary walls and mesangial expansion. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals granular deposition of IgG and C3 along the glomerular capillary walls and in the mesangium. Electron microscopy demonstrates subendothelial and mesangial electron-dense deposits, along with diffuse thickening of the glomerular basement membrane with a "tram-track" appearance. C3 and C4 levels in serum are low.

Which of the following pathological findings is most characteristic of this patient's condition?

(A) Linear deposition of IgG along the glomerular basement membrane.
(B) Subepithelial electron-dense deposits ("humps") on the epithelial side of the GBM.
(C) Paucity of neutrophils and monocytes within the glomeruli.
(D) Mesangial IgA deposition with minimal or no systemic immune complex deposition.
(E) Diffuse thickening of the glomerular basement membrane with a "tram-track" appearance on electron microscopy.

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 28-year-old male.
- **Symptoms:** Insidious onset lower extremity edema, dark urine (suggesting hematuria/proteinuria).
- **Signs:** Bilateral pitting edema, hypertension (150/95 mmHg).
- **Labs:** Proteinuria (3+), microscopic hematuria (20 RBCs/hpf), elevated creatinine (1.8 mg/dL), low albumin (2.5 g/dL), low C3 and C4 levels. This constellation points towards nephrotic/nephritic syndrome with complement consumption.
- **Biopsy Findings:**
    - **Light Microscopy:** Hypercellular glomeruli, thickened capillary walls, mesangial expansion.
    - **Immunofluorescence:** Granular IgG and C3 deposition along capillary walls and mesangium.
    - **Electron Microscopy:** Subendothelial and mesangial electron-dense deposits, diffuse GBM thickening, "tram-track" appearance.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most characteristic* pathological finding.

The clinical presentation (nephrotic/nephritic syndrome, hypertension, complement consumption) combined with the biopsy findings (hypercellular glomeruli, thickened GBM, granular immune deposits, subendothelial/mesangial deposits, low C3/C4) strongly suggests Membranoproliferative Glomerulonephritis (MPGN), specifically Type II (Dense Deposit Disease) or Type III, often associated with complement dysregulation. The "tram-track" appearance on electron microscopy due to GBM splitting and mesangial interposition is a hallmark feature of MPGN.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **MPGN Pathogenesis:** MPGN involves immune complex deposition and/or complement pathway dysregulation within the glomeruli.
    - **Type I (Immune Complex Mediated):** Associated with systemic diseases (e.g., SLE, infections) or primary. Immune complexes deposit in the mesangium and subendothelial space, activating complement.
    - **Type II (Dense Deposit Disease - DDD):** Caused by dysregulation of the alternative complement pathway, often due to autoantibodies against C3 convertase (C3NeF) or mutations in complement regulatory proteins (e.g., Factor H). Leads to massive C3 deposition in the GBM, forming dense deposits.
    - **Type III (Alternative Pathway C3 Glomerulonephritis):** Similar to Type II but without dense deposits; involves alternative pathway activation.
- **Morphological Features:**
    - **Light Microscopy:** Glomerular hypercellularity (mesangial and endothelial cells), thickening of capillary walls, mesangial expansion.
    - **Immunofluorescence:** Granular deposition of C3 (often dominant), IgG, IgM, C1q.
    - **Electron Microscopy:** Subendothelial and mesangial electron-dense deposits. Diffuse thickening of the GBM, often with splitting and duplication of the lamina densa, creating the characteristic "tram-track" appearance.
- **Complement Levels:** Often low C3 and C4 (especially in Type I and Type II), but C3 may be normal or high in Type III. The vignette states low C3 and C4, consistent with MPGN Type I or II.
- **"Tram-track" Appearance:** This specific EM finding results from the deposition of material (immune complexes, complement components) within the GBM, causing it to thicken and split, with mesangial matrix interposing between the split layers, resembling railroad tracks. This is a key diagnostic feature of MPGN.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Linear deposition of IgG along the glomerular basement membrane:** This is characteristic of Anti-Glomerular Basement Membrane (Anti-GBM) disease (Goodpasture syndrome), which typically presents with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and pulmonary hemorrhage. The immunofluorescence pattern is distinctly linear, not granular as described in the vignette.
- (B) **Subepithelial electron-dense deposits ("humps") on the epithelial side of the GBM:** This is the classic finding in Post-Streptococcal Glomerulonephritis (PSGN). PSGN typically presents acutely after a streptococcal infection, often in children, with nephritic syndrome. The deposits are located on the epithelial side of the GBM. The vignette describes subendothelial and mesangial deposits and a chronic presentation.
- (C) **Paucity of neutrophils and monocytes within the glomeruli:** This finding is characteristic of Minimal Change Disease (MCD) on light microscopy. MCD typically presents with nephrotic syndrome, especially in children, and has normal glomeruli on light microscopy, negative immunofluorescence, and effacement of podocyte foot processes on electron microscopy. The vignette describes hypercellular glomeruli with significant deposits.
- (D) **Mesangial IgA deposition with minimal or no systemic immune complex deposition:** This is the hallmark of IgA Nephropathy (Berger's disease). IgA nephropathy is the most common primary glomerulonephritis worldwide, often presenting with recurrent macroscopic hematuria following upper respiratory infections. While it involves mesangial deposits, the dominant immunoglobulin is IgA, and the characteristic "tram-track" appearance is not seen.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                     | MPGN (Vignette)                                  | Anti-GBM Disease (A)          | PSGN (B)                       | MCD (C)                         | IgA Nephropathy (D)              |
| :-------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :------------------------------ | :------------------------------- |
| **Clinical Presentation**    | Nephrotic/Nephritic, Edema, HTN, Chronic         | RPGN, Hemoptysis, Acute       | Acute Nephritic Syndrome        | Nephrotic Syndrome, Acute        | Recurrent Hematuria, Chronic     |
| **Light Microscopy**         | Hypercellular, Thickened Cap Walls, Mesangial Exp | Normal or Crescentic Glomeruli | Hypercellular Glomeruli        | Normal Glomeruli                 | Mesangial Hypercellularity       |
| **Immunofluorescence**       | Granular IgG, C3 (Cap Walls, Mesangium)          | Linear IgG along GBM          | Granular IgG, C3 ("Humps")      | Negative                        | Mesangial IgA                    |
| **Electron Microscopy**      | Subendothelial/Mesangial Deposits, "Tram-track" GBM | No Deposits                  | Subepithelial "Humps"          | Foot Process Effacement         | Mesangial IgA Deposits           |
| **Complement Levels**        | Low C3, C4 (often)                               | Normal                       | Low C3 (transiently)           | Normal                          | Normal                           |
| **Key Pathognomonic Feature** | "Tram-track" GBM                                | Linear IgG                    | Subepithelial "Humps"          | Foot Process Effacement          | Mesangial IgA                    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN) is characterized by glomerular hypercellularity, thickened capillary walls, and mesangial expansion, with immune complex and/or complement deposition. The "tram-track" appearance on electron microscopy due to GBM splitting is a key diagnostic feature. Differentiate MPGN from other glomerulonephritides based on immunofluorescence patterns (linear vs. granular), deposit location (subepithelial vs. subendothelial/mesangial), and specific immunoglobulin deposition (IgA). Remember the association with complement pathway dysregulation and low C3/C4 levels.

<a id='question-147'></a>

### Question 147: Renal Pathology: IgA Nephropathy
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: IgA nephropathy (Berger's disease) is characterized by the deposition of IgA immune complexes in the glomerular mesangium, leading to mesangial proliferation and glomerular damage. The definitive diagnosis relies on demonstrating mesangial IgA deposition via immunofluorescence microscopy of a renal biopsy. (IgA肾病（伯杰病）的特征是IgA免疫复合物沉积在肾小球系膜中，导致系膜增生和肾小球损伤。确诊依赖于通过肾活检的免疫荧光显微镜检查证明系膜中存在IgA沉积。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department 3 days after experiencing macroscopic hematuria following an upper respiratory tract infection. He reports feeling generally well otherwise, with no significant past medical history, including no history of hypertension, diabetes, or systemic autoimmune diseases. His vital signs are stable. Urinalysis reveals persistent microscopic hematuria (50 RBCs/hpf) and mild proteinuria (1+). Serum creatinine is 1.2 mg/dL (normal range 0.6-1.2 mg/dL), and serum albumin is 4.0 g/dL (normal range 3.5-5.0 g/dL). A renal biopsy is performed. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows granular deposits of IgA within the glomerular mesangium. Light microscopy reveals mesangial hypercellularity and endocapillary proliferation. Electron microscopy shows electron-dense deposits localized primarily within the mesangium.

Which of the following findings is most characteristic of this patient's condition?

(A) Subepithelial immune complex deposits ("humps") seen on electron microscopy.
(B) Linear deposition of IgG along the glomerular basement membrane seen on immunofluorescence microscopy.
(C) Presence of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies in the serum.
(D) Mesangial IgA deposition seen on immunofluorescence microscopy.
(E) Paucity of immune deposits on immunofluorescence microscopy.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** Young adult male (32 years old).
- **Presenting Symptom:** Macroscopic hematuria following an upper respiratory tract infection (synpharyngitic hematuria). This is a classic trigger for IgA nephropathy.
- **Urinalysis:** Microscopic hematuria and mild proteinuria. Consistent with glomerular disease.
- **Renal Function:** Serum creatinine is at the upper limit of normal (1.2 mg/dL), suggesting mild renal impairment.
- **Biopsy Findings:**
    - **Immunofluorescence (IF):** Granular deposits of IgA within the glomerular mesangium. This is the hallmark diagnostic finding for IgA nephropathy.
    - **Light Microscopy (LM):** Mesangial hypercellularity and endocapillary proliferation. Common findings in IgA nephropathy.
    - **Electron Microscopy (EM):** Electron-dense deposits localized primarily within the mesangium. Correlates with the IF findings.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most characteristic finding of this patient's condition, given the clinical presentation and biopsy results.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **IgA Nephropathy Pathogenesis:** The exact pathogenesis is not fully understood, but it involves the abnormal glycosylation of IgA1, leading to the formation of polymeric IgA1 complexes that deposit in the glomerular mesangium. These deposits trigger complement activation (alternative pathway) and inflammation, leading to mesangial cell proliferation, matrix expansion, and eventual glomerular sclerosis and renal failure.
- **Diagnostic Hallmark:** The defining feature of IgA nephropathy is the presence of IgA deposits in the mesangium, detected by immunofluorescence microscopy of a renal biopsy. This finding, combined with the typical clinical presentation (synpharyngitic hematuria, often in young adults), is diagnostic.
- **Biopsy Findings Correlation:** The light microscopy findings (mesangial hypercellularity, endocapillary proliferation) and electron microscopy findings (mesangial electron-dense deposits) are consistent with the immunofluorescence findings of mesangial IgA deposition.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Subepithelial immune complex deposits ("humps") seen on electron microscopy:** This finding is characteristic of post-infectious glomerulonephritis (PIGN), typically caused by Group A Streptococcus. PIGN often presents with hematuria and edema following a streptococcal infection, but the immune deposits are subepithelial, not primarily mesangial, and typically contain IgG and C3.
- (B) **Linear deposition of IgG along the glomerular basement membrane seen on immunofluorescence microscopy:** This pattern is pathognomonic for anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) disease (Goodpasture syndrome). Anti-GBM disease presents with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and often pulmonary hemorrhage. The deposits are linear because the antibodies target the entire GBM.
- (C) **Presence of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies in the serum:** As explained in (B), anti-GBM antibodies are characteristic of anti-GBM disease, not IgA nephropathy. IgA nephropathy is characterized by IgA deposition, not anti-GBM antibodies.
- (E) **Paucity of immune deposits on immunofluorescence microscopy:** This finding is characteristic of pauci-immune glomerulonephritis, such as ANCA-associated vasculitis (e.g., granulomatosis with polyangiitis, microscopic polyangiitis). These conditions typically present with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and systemic vasculitic symptoms. IgA nephropathy is defined by the *presence* of IgA deposits, not their absence.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                     | IgA Nephropathy (Berger's Disease) | Post-Infectious GN (PIGN) | Anti-GBM Disease (Goodpasture's) | Pauci-Immune GN (ANCA Vasculitis) |
| :-------------------------- | :--------------------------------- | :----------------------- | :------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| **Typical Age**             | Young Adult                       | Children, Young Adults   | Middle-Aged                     | Middle-Aged                      |
| **Clinical Presentation**   | Synpharyngitic Hematuria          | Hematuria, Edema         | RPGN, Pulmonary Hemorrhage      | RPGN                              |
| **Immune Deposits (IF)**    | Mesangial IgA                     | Subepithelial IgG, C3    | Linear IgG along GBM             | Pauci-immune                     |
| **Electron Microscopy (EM)** | Mesangial electron-dense deposits | Subepithelial "humps"    | None (or GBM thickening)        | None                              |
| **Serum Antibodies**        | None specific                     | Anti-streptolysin O      | Anti-GBM antibodies              | ANCA                              |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
IgA nephropathy is the most common primary glomerulonephritis worldwide, characterized by IgA deposition in the mesangium. Remember the classic presentation of synpharyngitic hematuria in a young adult. Differentiating it from PIGN (subepithelial humps), Anti-GBM disease (linear IgG), and Pauci-immune GN (no deposits) relies on understanding the location and type of immune deposits seen on renal biopsy.

<a id='question-148'></a>

### Question 148: Renal Pathology: Focus 03-48 (Membranoproliferative Glomerulonephritis - MPGN)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN) is characterized by glomerular basement membrane (GBM) thickening, mesangial hypercellularity, and endocapillary proliferation. It is classified into types I, II, and III based on underlying etiology and immune complex deposition patterns. Type I (immune complex-mediated) involves subendothelial and mesangial deposits, often associated with systemic diseases or infections. Type II (dense deposit disease, DDD) involves dense deposits within the GBM itself, typically due to dysregulation of the alternative complement pathway. The question tests the ability to differentiate between MPGN types based on histological and ultrastructural findings, particularly the presence of dense deposits.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male presents with insidious onset of nephrotic syndrome, including generalized edema, proteinuria (5g/day), and hypoalbuminemia. He denies recent infections or systemic symptoms like joint pain or rash. Renal biopsy reveals thickened glomerular basement membranes (GBMs) with a "tram-track" appearance on light microscopy. Immunofluorescence microscopy shows granular deposition of C3 along the GBM and in the mesangium, with minimal or no IgG or IgM deposition. Electron microscopy demonstrates numerous large, electron-dense deposits within the GBM, splitting the lamina densa and extending into the subendothelial space. There is also evidence of mesangial cell proliferation and endocapillary hypercellularity. C3 levels in the serum are low. Which of the following findings is most characteristic of this patient's condition?

(A) Subepithelial immune complex deposits with "spikes" on the GBM.
(B) Linear deposition of IgG along the GBM.
(C) Subendothelial immune complex deposits with mesangial proliferation and "wire loops".
(D) Dense deposits within the lamina densa of the GBM, associated with alternative complement pathway dysregulation.
(E) Pauci-immune crescentic glomerulonephritis with fibrinoid necrosis.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 35-year-old male with nephrotic syndrome (edema, proteinuria, hypoalbuminemia). This points towards glomerular disease affecting the filtration barrier.
- **Light Microscopy:** Thickened GBMs with "tram-track" appearance. This is a classic finding in MPGN, indicating GBM duplication or splitting.
- **Immunofluorescence:** Granular C3 deposition along GBM and mesangium, minimal IgG/IgM. This suggests C3-dominant immune complex deposition, characteristic of MPGN Type I or Type II (DDD). The lack of significant IgG/IgM makes Type III (anti-GBM disease) less likely.
- **Electron Microscopy:** Large, electron-dense deposits *within* the GBM (lamina densa), splitting it. This is the defining ultrastructural feature of MPGN Type II, also known as Dense Deposit Disease (DDD). Mesangial proliferation and endocapillary hypercellularity are also consistent with MPGN.
- **Serum C3:** Low levels. Chronic activation of the alternative complement pathway, often seen in DDD, consumes C3, leading to low serum levels.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most characteristic* finding. Given the specific EM findings (dense deposits within the lamina densa) and low C3, this points strongly towards MPGN Type II (DDD).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **MPGN Type II (DDD):** This condition is characterized by the deposition of dense, electron-dense material within the lamina densa of the glomerular basement membrane. This deposition is not typically composed of classical immune complexes (IgG, IgM) but rather involves complement components (especially C3) and other plasma proteins.
- **Pathogenesis:** The primary defect in most cases of DDD is dysregulation of the alternative complement pathway, often due to autoantibodies against complement regulatory proteins like Factor H (CFH) or C3 nephritic factor (C3NeF), which is a C3 convertase that cleaves C3 continuously. This leads to uncontrolled activation of C3, generating C3bBbP (alternative pathway C5 convertase) and C5b-9 (membrane attack complex), resulting in inflammation and damage.
- **Histology:** Light microscopy shows thickened GBMs ("tram-track" appearance), mesangial hypercellularity, and endocapillary proliferation.
- **Immunofluorescence:** Strong granular C3 deposition, often with weaker C1q deposition. IgG and IgM are typically absent or minimal.
- **Electron Microscopy:** The hallmark is the presence of large, homogeneous, electron-dense deposits within the lamina densa, often splitting it.
- **Clinical Features:** Often presents with nephrotic syndrome, nephritic syndrome, or mixed features. Low serum C3 levels are common due to chronic complement consumption.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Subepithelial immune complex deposits with "spikes" on the GBM. This describes Membranous Nephropathy, characterized by IgG and C3 deposition on the epithelial side of the GBM, leading to GBM thickening and spike formation. The EM findings (subepithelial deposits) and IF findings (prominent IgG) are inconsistent with the vignette.
- (B): Linear deposition of IgG along the GBM. This is characteristic of Anti-Glomerular Basement Membrane (Anti-GBM) disease (Goodpasture syndrome), where antibodies directly target the GBM. The IF pattern is linear, not granular, and the EM shows no deposits. The clinical presentation is typically rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis (RPGN) or pulmonary hemorrhage.
- (C): Subendothelial immune complex deposits with mesangial proliferation and "wire loops". This describes Lupus Nephritis (Class IV), characterized by subendothelial immune complex deposition, mesangial proliferation, and GBM thickening ("wire loops" on EM). IF shows granular deposition of IgG, IgM, IgA, C3, and C1q. While MPGN Type I also has subendothelial deposits, the *dense deposits within the lamina densa* seen on EM in the vignette are specific to Type II (DDD).
- (E): Pauci-immune crescentic glomerulonephritis with fibrinoid necrosis. This describes ANCA-associated vasculitis (e.g., Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis, Microscopic Polyangiitis). It is characterized by minimal or no immune deposits (pauci-immune), crescent formation, and often fibrinoid necrosis. The IF findings (minimal deposits) and EM findings (no dense deposits) are inconsistent with the vignette.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | MPGN Type II (DDD)                                  | Membranous Nephropathy                     | Anti-GBM Disease                      | Lupus Nephritis (Class IV)              | ANCA-associated GN                     |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------- |
| **Light Microscopy**    | Tram-track GBM, mesangial/endocapillary proliferation | Thickened GBM (spikes), no/mild cellularity | Normal or mildly thickened GBM         | Wire loops, mesangial/endocapillary proliferation | Crescents, fibrinoid necrosis, pauci-immune |
| **Immunofluorescence** | Granular C3 (strong), minimal IgG/IgM                | Granular IgG, C3 (subepithelial)          | Linear IgG (along GBM)                | Granular IgG, IgM, IgA, C3, C1q        | Pauci-immune (minimal deposits)        |
| **Electron Microscopy** | Dense deposits *within* lamina densa                 | Subepithelial deposits, GBM spikes        | No deposits                            | Subendothelial deposits, wire loops    | No significant deposits                |
| **Serum C3**            | Low (often)                                         | Normal (usually)                           | Normal (usually)                       | Normal (usually)                       | Normal (usually)                       |
| **Pathogenesis**        | Alternative complement dysregulation                | Anti-PLA2R antibodies (most common)       | Anti-GBM antibodies                   | Immune complex deposition             | ANCA-associated vasculitis             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN) Type II, or Dense Deposit Disease (DDD), is uniquely characterized by electron-dense deposits within the glomerular basement membrane lamina densa, often associated with alternative complement pathway dysregulation and low serum C3. Differentiating MPGN Type II from other glomerular diseases requires careful attention to ultrastructural findings on electron microscopy. Key differentials include Membranous Nephropathy (subepithelial deposits, IgG spikes), Anti-GBM disease (linear IgG IF), Lupus Nephritis (subendothelial deposits, wire loops, mixed IF), and ANCA-associated GN (pauci-immune crescents).

<a id='question-149'></a>

### Question 149: Renal Pathology: Focus 03-49 (MPGN type II is now called dense deposit)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the pathological features and diagnostic methods for Dense Deposit Disease (DDD), previously known as Membranoproliferative Glomerulonephritis (MPGN) type II, specifically focusing on the characteristic electron microscopy findings and the associated complement pathway dysregulation. (问题考察的是致密沉积病 (DDD)，以前称为膜性增生性肾小球肾炎 (MPGN) II 型的病理特征和诊断方法，特别是特征性电子显微镜发现和相关的补体途径功能障碍。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male presents to the clinic complaining of insidious onset of lower extremity edema and dark urine over the past 6 months. He denies recent infections, fever, or joint pain. His past medical history is unremarkable except for hypertension diagnosed 2 years ago, currently managed with lisinopril. Physical examination reveals bilateral pitting edema up to the knees and blood pressure of 160/100 mmHg. Urinalysis shows 3+ proteinuria and microscopic hematuria (10-15 RBCs/hpf). Serum creatinine is elevated at 2.5 mg/dL (reference range: 0.6-1.2 mg/dL), and serum albumin is low at 2.8 g/dL (reference range: 3.5-5.0 g/dL). A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy shows diffuse mesangial hypercellularity and endocapillary proliferation. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals diffuse granular deposition of C3 along the glomerular basement membrane (GBM) and in the mesangium, with negative staining for IgG and IgA. Electron microscopy reveals numerous subendothelial and intramembranous electron-dense deposits within the GBM, giving it a "tram-track" appearance. C3 levels in the serum are markedly decreased.

Which of the following findings is most characteristic of this patient's renal biopsy?

(A) Subepithelial electron-dense deposits along the GBM, often described as "spikes".
(B) Linear deposition of IgG along the GBM.
(C) Subendothelial electron-dense deposits, typically seen in lupus nephritis class IV.
(D) Electron-dense deposits within the mesangium and subepithelial space, forming "humps".
(E) Electron-dense deposits within the GBM, creating a "tram-track" appearance due to duplication of the glomerular basement membrane.

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 35-year-old male with insidious onset edema, dark urine, hypertension, proteinuria, hematuria, elevated creatinine, and low albumin, suggesting nephrotic syndrome with renal insufficiency.
- **Light Microscopy:** Diffuse mesangial hypercellularity and endocapillary proliferation are characteristic of proliferative glomerulonephritis.
- **Immunofluorescence:** Diffuse granular C3 deposition without significant IgG or IgA points towards a C3-mediated glomerulopathy.
- **Electron Microscopy:** "Tram-track" appearance of the GBM due to subendothelial and intramembranous electron-dense deposits is the pathognomonic finding for Dense Deposit Disease (DDD).
- **Serum C3:** Markedly decreased C3 levels are typical in DDD due to chronic complement activation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most characteristic finding on the renal biopsy, specifically related to electron microscopy.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Dense Deposit Disease (DDD):** Formerly MPGN type II. It is a C3 glomerulopathy characterized by dysregulation of the alternative complement pathway, leading to excessive C3 activation and deposition within the glomeruli.
- **Pathogenesis:** Most cases (80-90%) are caused by autoantibodies against the C3 convertase (C3bBb), leading to its stabilization and continuous activation. Other causes include mutations in complement regulatory genes (e.g., Factor H, Factor I, MCP).
- **Light Microscopy:** Shows mesangial expansion, endocapillary proliferation, and sometimes crescent formation.
- **Immunofluorescence:** Diffuse granular staining for C3 along the GBM and in the mesangium. IgG and IgA are typically negative or minimal.
- **Electron Microscopy:** The hallmark is the presence of electron-dense deposits within the glomerular basement membrane, specifically in the subendothelial space and intramembranously. These deposits cause thickening of the GBM and duplication of the lamina densa, resulting in the characteristic "tram-track" appearance. This is distinct from other forms of glomerulonephritis.
- **Clinical Features:** Often presents with nephrotic syndrome, nephritic syndrome, or mixed features. Chronic kidney disease is common. Low serum C3 levels are characteristic.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Subepithelial electron-dense deposits along the GBM, often described as "spikes" (due to GBM material growing between the deposits), are characteristic of Membranous Nephropathy. This condition typically presents with nephrotic syndrome and involves IgG deposition on IF. This is incorrect because the vignette describes C3 deposition and "tram-track" appearance.
- (B): Linear deposition of IgG along the GBM is the hallmark of Anti-Glomerular Basement Membrane (Anti-GBM) disease (Goodpasture syndrome). This typically presents as rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and/or pulmonary hemorrhage. This is incorrect because the vignette describes granular C3 deposition and electron-dense deposits within the GBM, not linear IgG.
- (C): Subendothelial electron-dense deposits are seen in various conditions, including lupus nephritis (especially class III and IV), post-infectious glomerulonephritis, and membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN) type I. However, the "tram-track" appearance specifically due to intramembranous and subendothelial deposits is characteristic of DDD, not just subendothelial deposits alone. Furthermore, lupus nephritis typically shows significant IgG and C3 deposition. This is incorrect because the vignette specifically describes the "tram-track" appearance and C3-dominant deposition.
- (D): Electron-dense deposits within the mesangium and subepithelial space, forming "humps," are characteristic of Acute Post-Streptococcal Glomerulonephritis (APSGN). APSGN typically presents after a streptococcal infection, shows granular IgG and C3 deposition, and often resolves completely. This is incorrect because the vignette describes deposits within the GBM ("tram-track") and C3-dominant deposition, not mesangial/subepithelial humps.
- (E): Electron-dense deposits within the GBM, creating a "tram-track" appearance due to duplication of the glomerular basement membrane, is the defining ultrastructural feature of Dense Deposit Disease (DDD). This perfectly matches the description in the vignette's electron microscopy findings.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Dense Deposit Disease (DDD) | Membranous Nephropathy | Anti-GBM Disease | Lupus Nephritis (Class IV) | Post-Streptococcal GN |
|----------------------|-----------------------------|------------------------|-------------------|-----------------------------|------------------------|
| **Light Microscopy** | Mesangial/Endocapillary Proliferation | GBM Thickening, Effacement of Podocytes | Crescent Formation, Fibrinoid Necrosis | Subendothelial Deposits, "Wire Loops" | Hypercellularity, "Humps" |
| **IF Microscopy**    | Diffuse C3, +/- C1q, -IgG/IgA | Diffuse IgG, C3       | Linear IgG        | Granular IgG, C3, +/- IgA, C1q | Granular IgG, C3       |
| **EM Microscopy**    | Intramembranous/Subendothelial Deposits, "Tram-Track" GBM | Subepithelial Deposits, "Spikes" | No deposits (GBM damage) | Subendothelial Deposits | Subepithelial "Humps" |
| **Serum C3**         | Low                         | Normal                 | Normal            | Low/Normal                  | Low                    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Dense Deposit Disease (DDD) is a C3 glomerulopathy characterized by dysregulation of the alternative complement pathway. The pathognomonic finding on electron microscopy is the "tram-track" appearance of the glomerular basement membrane due to the presence of electron-dense deposits within the GBM itself (intramembranous) and beneath the endothelium (subendothelial). Key differentials based on EM include membranous nephropathy (subepithelial spikes), anti-GBM disease (no deposits), lupus nephritis (subendothelial deposits), and post-infectious GN (subepithelial humps). Always correlate EM findings with IF and light microscopy for accurate diagnosis.

<a id='question-150'></a>

### Question 150: Renal Pathology: Focus 03-50 (disease, and is increasingly considered its)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology, clinical presentation, and diagnostic features of IgA nephropathy (Berger's disease), specifically focusing on its association with Henoch-Schönlein purpura (HSP) and the characteristic finding on renal biopsy. (IgA肾病 (伯杰病) 的病理生理、临床表现和诊断特征，特别是其与亨克-肖恩莱茵紫癜 (HSP) 的关联以及肾活检中的特征性发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old male presents to the emergency department with gross hematuria following an upper respiratory tract infection two weeks prior. He reports experiencing mild flank pain but denies fever, chills, or dysuria. His past medical history is unremarkable, and he takes no medications. Physical examination reveals mild edema in the lower extremities. Urinalysis shows macroscopic hematuria with dysmorphic red blood cells and proteinuria (1.5 g/day). Serum creatinine is 1.8 mg/dL (reference range: 0.6-1.2 mg/dL), and serum IgA levels are elevated. A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy shows diffuse mesangial proliferation and endocapillary hypercellularity. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals granular deposits of IgA in the mesangium. Electron microscopy shows electron-dense deposits in the mesangium. Which of the following is the most likely underlying condition associated with this patient's renal biopsy findings?

(A) Post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis
(B) Membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN)
(C) Henoch-Schönlein purpura (HSP)
(D) Minimal change disease
(E) Anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) disease

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 28-year-old male. IgA nephropathy is common in young adults.
*   **Presenting Symptom:** Gross hematuria following an upper respiratory tract infection (URI). This is a classic trigger for IgA nephropathy, often occurring 1-3 days after a URI (synpharyngitic hematuria).
*   **Associated Symptoms:** Mild flank pain, lower extremity edema. Suggests renal involvement and potential fluid overload/proteinuria.
*   **Urinalysis:** Macroscopic hematuria, dysmorphic RBCs (suggesting glomerular origin), proteinuria (1.5 g/day). Consistent with glomerulonephritis.
*   **Labs:** Elevated serum creatinine (1.8 mg/dL) indicates acute kidney injury. Elevated serum IgA levels are characteristic of IgA nephropathy.
*   **Renal Biopsy Findings:**
    *   **Light Microscopy:** Diffuse mesangial proliferation and endocapillary hypercellularity. Typical findings in IgA nephropathy.
    *   **Immunofluorescence:** Granular deposits of IgA in the mesangium. This is the pathognomonic finding for IgA nephropathy.
    *   **Electron Microscopy:** Electron-dense deposits in the mesangium. Correlates with the IgA deposits seen on immunofluorescence.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely *underlying condition* associated with these findings. The biopsy findings are diagnostic of IgA nephropathy. IgA nephropathy is the renal manifestation of Henoch-Schönlein purpura (HSP).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **IgA Nephropathy Pathophysiology:** Characterized by the deposition of IgA-containing immune complexes in the glomerular mesangium. This triggers inflammation, mesangial cell proliferation, and eventual glomerular damage. The exact trigger is unknown, but infections (especially URIs) are common preceding events. Elevated serum IgA is often present.
*   **HSP Pathophysiology:** A systemic small-vessel vasculitis characterized by IgA deposition in vessel walls. It typically presents with palpable purpura (especially on lower extremities and buttocks), arthritis/arthralgia, abdominal pain, and renal involvement (HSP nephritis). HSP nephritis is histologically identical to IgA nephropathy. IgA nephropathy is considered the renal-limited form of HSP, or HSP is considered the systemic form with IgA nephropathy as the renal component.
*   **Biopsy Correlation:** The described biopsy findings (mesangial IgA deposition) are the hallmark of IgA nephropathy, which is the renal manifestation of HSP.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis (PSGN):** Typically occurs 1-3 weeks after a streptococcal infection (pharyngitis or impetigo). Biopsy shows diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis with subepithelial "humps" on EM and granular deposits of IgG and C3 (not IgA) on IF. While it can present with hematuria after infection, the specific IgA deposition rules it out.
*   **(B) Membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN):** Characterized by thickening of the glomerular basement membrane (GBM) and mesangial proliferation. IF shows granular deposits of C3, often with IgG, in a "tram-track" pattern along the GBM. EM shows subendothelial and mesangial deposits. IgA deposition is not the primary feature.
*   **(D) Minimal change disease (MCD):** The most common cause of nephrotic syndrome in children. Light microscopy is normal. IF is negative. EM shows effacement of podocyte foot processes. It does not typically present with gross hematuria or IgA deposition.
*   **(E) Anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) disease (Goodpasture syndrome):** Characterized by linear deposition of IgG along the GBM on IF. Presents with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and often pulmonary hemorrhage. IgA deposition is absent.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | IgA Nephropathy / HSP Nephritis | PSGN                     | MPGN                     | MCD                       | Anti-GBM Disease          |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------- | :------------------------ | :------------------------ |
| **Typical Age**         | Young adults                   | Children, young adults   | Variable                 | Children                  | Young-middle aged adults |
| **Preceding Event**     | URI (common)                   | Strep infection          | Variable                 | None specific             | None specific             |
| **Presentation**        | Hematuria (synpharyngitic)     | Hematuria, edema         | Nephrotic/Nephritic      | Nephrotic syndrome        | RPGN, pulm hemorrhage     |
| **Light Microscopy**    | Mesangial proliferation        | Diffuse proliferation    | Mesangial proliferation, GBM thickening | Normal                    | Crescent formation        |
| **Immunofluorescence**  | **Mesangial IgA**              | Subepithelial IgG, C3    | C3, IgG (tram-track)     | Negative                  | **Linear IgG along GBM** |
| **Electron Microscopy** | Mesangial deposits            | Subepithelial "humps"    | Subendothelial, mesangial | Foot process effacement   | None (or GBM breaks)      |
| **Systemic Features**   | Purpura, arthritis, abd pain (HSP) | None                    | None                    | None                      | Pulmonary hemorrhage      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
IgA nephropathy is characterized by mesangial IgA deposition on renal biopsy, often presenting with hematuria following an infection. It is the renal manifestation of Henoch-Schönlein purpura (HSP), a systemic IgA vasculitis. Differentiating it from other glomerulonephritides relies heavily on the specific immunofluorescence pattern (IgA vs. IgG/C3 vs. linear IgG). Key differentials include PSGN (IgG/C3, post-strep), MPGN (C3, tram-track), MCD (normal LM/IF, foot process effacement), and anti-GBM disease (linear IgG).
